High-Dimensional Sparse Clustering via Iterative Semidefinite Programming Relaxed K-Means
Jongmin Mun, Paromita Dubey, Yingying Fan

Abstract
We propose an iterative algorithm for clustering high-dimensional data, where the true signal lies in a much lower-dimensional space. Our method alternates between feature selection and clustering, without requiring precise estimation of sparse model parameters. Feature selection is performed by thresholding a rough estimate of the discriminative direction, while clustering is carried out via a semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxation of K-means. In the isotropic case, the algorithm is motivated by the minimax separation bound for exact recovery of cluster labels using varying sparse subsets of features. This bound highlights the critical role of variable selection in achieving exact recovery. We further extend the algorithm to settings with unknown sparse precision matrices, avoiding full model parameter estimation by computing only the minimally required quantities. Across a range of simulation settings, we find that the proposed iterative approach outperforms several state-of-the-art methods, especially in higher dimensions.



Keywords
:
exact recovery; Gaussian mixture models; high-dimensional data; K-means; precision matrix; semidefinite relaxation; sparsity; unsupervised learning; variable selection

†
†

1
Introduction

High-dimensional clustering algorithms group unlabeled observations when the number of features exceeds or matches the sample size. It is widely applied to data from advanced recording technologies, such as
neurophysiological recordings
(
Cunningham and Yu, 2014
;
Kadir et al., 2014
;
Park et al., 2024
)
,
gene expression
(
Bittner et al., 2000
;
Curtis et al., 2012
;
He et al., 2021
)
,
and neuroimaging
(
Eshaghi et al., 2021
;
Heller et al., 2006
;
Poulakis et al., 2022
)
,
which produce high-dimensional measurements but limited samples due to acquisition costs. In these settings, typically only a small subset of features is relevant for clustering.
Then, a key challenge lies in the interdependence between the two unsupervised tasks of variable selection and clustering.
On one hand, with unknown sparsity, including too many noise features in the clustering algorithm hinders the results. On the other hand, it is challenging to pinpoint the signal features when the true cluster structure is unknown. This motivates an iterative algorithm that alternates between clustering and variable selection until convergence leading to the identification of the true clusters.

Sparse high-dimensional clustering model.

To effectively study the role of sparsity, we assume a Gaussian mixture model with sparse discriminative directions.
Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
denote the data matrix,
where
p
may exceed
n
.
Its columns
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
are independently generated as

\mathbf{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})

if
i\in G_{k}^{\ast}
,
where the non-random sets
G_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,G_{K}^{\ast}
form a partition of

\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
The number of clusters
K
is fixed and known. The unknown cluster centers are denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is the common covariance matrix.
Let us denote the true precision matrix as
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}:=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})^{-1}
.
We consider both cases where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
is known and where it is unknown but have a sparse structure. Our objective is to recover
G_{1}^{\ast},\dots,G_{K}^{\ast}
while treating
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}
’s and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}
as nuisance parameters.
We assume that only a small, unknown subset of features, indexed by
S_{0}
, is relevant for clustering. Formally,

S_{0}:=\bigcup_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\mathrm{supp}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})\bigr)\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}

with
|S_{0}|\ll p
.
This assumption aligns with results from classification of two-class Gaussian mixtures, where the optimal decision boundary is proportional to
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})
, maximizing the signal-to-noise ratio
(
Fan et al., 2013
)
.

Previous methods.

High-dimensional sparse clustering methods roughly fall into two types: two-step and iterative.
We start with the two-step type.
Since clustering after conventional dimension reduction, such as PCA
(
Ghosh and Chinnaiyan, 2002
;
Liu et al., 2003
;
Tamayo et al., 2007
)
, can miss a sparse clustering signal
(
Chang, 1983
)
,
many methods adopt (Gaussian mixture) models to
select features based on the clustering signal.
They select features using multiple testing
based on goodness-of-fit
(
Jin and Wang, 2016
)
or moments
(
Azizyan et al., 2013
;
Verzelen and Arias-Castro, 2017
)
,
model selection
(
Maugis et al., 2009
;
Raftery and Dean, 2006
)
,
sparse PCA
(
Löffler et al., 2022
)
,
or an estimated sparse discriminating direction (denoted
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
; see Section
3.1
)
(
Azizyan et al., 2015
)
.
More examples include
Bouveyron and Brunet-Saumard, 2014
;
Brodinová et al., 2019
;
Kadir et al., 2014
;
Liu et al., 2023
.
Since it is difficult to identify the signal features without knowing the cluster structure, two-step methods often require a stronger signal compared to iterative methods (compare the conditions in
Jin and Wang, 2016
with
Cai et al., 2019
).
On the other hand,
iterative methods repeatedly refine cluster assignments and model parameters estimates. Most methods alternate between clustering and regularized estimation of model parameters, such as feature weights
(
Chakraborty et al., 2023
;
Huo and Tseng, 2017
;
Li et al., 2018
;
Tsai and Chiu, 2008
;
Witten and Tibshirani, 2010
;
Yuan et al., 2024
)
, cluster centers
(
Sun et al., 2012
)
, and feature rankings
(
Arias-Castro and Pu, 2017
;
Zhang et al., 2020
)
.
Regularized EM algorithms follow the same principle: the E-step updates cluster assignments, while the M-step performs regularized estimation of sparse model parameters
(
Bouveyron et al., 2007
;
Dong et al., 2024
;
Fraley and Raftery, 2007
;
Fu et al., 2021
;
Guo et al., 2010
;
Pan and Shen, 2007
;
Wang and Zhu, 2008
;
Xie et al., 2008
;
Zhou et al., 2009
)
. Notably,
Cai et al., 2019
establish a minimax rate of order
(s\log p)/n
for the excess clustering risk on new samples, based on estimating
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
from the training data using
\ell_{1}
-regularized EM.
Sparse Bayesian hierarchical models
(
Tadesse et al., 2005
;
Yao et al., 2025
)
and sparse convex clustering
(
Chakraborty and and Xu, 2023
;
Wang et al., 2018
)
also follow the iterative paradigm, as their fitting procedures, such as MCMC or ADMM , progressively refine parameter estimates and feature selection.

Our contributions.

In the existing literature of iterative methods, clustering heavily depends on accuracy of estimated nuisance parameters such as cluster centers. Additionally, their estimation requires tuning sparsity-inducing regularization parameters or hyperpriors, which are challenging to tune in unsupervised problems.
In contrast, our method alternates between clustering and feature selection, where our feature selection step does not require precise estimation of nuisance parameters.
Features are selected by thresholding a rough estimate of the discriminating direction
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
;
this estimate is used solely to guide feature selection and has no direct impact on the clustering step. Based on the selected features, clustering is carried out using an SDP-relaxed K-means on a transformed dataset, avoiding accurate estimation of cluster centers or the covariance matrix. To obtain the transformed dataset, we adapt a high-dimensional precision matrix estimation tool
(
Fan and Lv, 2016
)
, modifying it to estimate only the minimally required quantities without estimating high dimensonal nuisance parameters.
In the case of a known isotropic covariance matrix, we motivate our algorithm by deriving a minimax separation bound for exact recovery under varying feature subsets by SDP
K
-means, which scales as
\log n+s\log p/n+\sqrt{s\log p/n}
, while the minimax lower bound scales as
\log n
. In the common high-dimensional regime where
s\log p/n\leq\log n
, the upper and lower bounds match.

Notation.

We denote vectors and matrices by boldface letters,
and their individual elements by the corresponding non-boldface letters.
For a matrix
\mathbf{A}
,
single and double subscripts denote columns and (row, column) elements, respectively.
A subscript may be a single index or an index set.
For vectors not derived from a matrix, a subscript denotes an element or a sub-vector. If the vector or matrix already contains a subscript, we use parentheses to avoid ambiguity, for example,
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{S}
.
We write
\mathbf{A}\geq 0
,
\mathbf{A}>0
, and
\mathbf{A}\succeq 0
to denote elementwise nonnegativity, elementwise positivity, and positive semidefiniteness of a matrix, respectively. For a set
S
, let
|S|
denotes its cardinality.
For a positive integer
a
, let
\mathbf{1}_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{a}
denote the
all-one vector
,
and
[a]
denote
\{1,\cdots,a\}
.
For a set
G_{k}\subset[n]
, let
\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
denote the indicator vector that takes the value 1 at indices in
G_{k}
and 0 elsewhere.

2
SDP relaxation of K-means and theoretical motivation

Section
2.1
presents the SDP relaxation of
K
-means
(
Peng and Wei, 2007
;
Zhuang et al., 2023
)
and its limitation in high dimensions due to its inability to adapt to sparsity, motivating our sparsity-aware extension. Section
2.2
provides theoretical motivation for the proposed approach, supporting iterative methods that solve multiple SDP relaxations over varying subsets of variables and highlighting the importance of sparsity.

2.1
SDP relexation of K-means problem and its limitation in high dimensions

It has been derived in
(
Peng and Wei, 2007
;
Zhuang et al., 2023
)
that finding the partition
G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}
that maximizes the K-means objective
can be formulated as the following NP-hard mixed-integer program
(
Mahajan et al., 2012
)
:

\max_{\mathbf{H}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}}\;\langle\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{H}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{H}^{\top}\rangle,\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{H}\mathbf{1}_{K}=\mathbf{1}_{n},

(1)

where
the inner product denotes the Frobenius inner product,

\mathbf{B}:=\mathrm{diag}(|G_{1}|,\ldots,|G_{K}|)
,
and
each row of
\mathbf{H}
is a one-hot cluster indicator, formally

H_{i,k}:=\mathbbm{1}(\mathbf{X}_{i}\in G_{k})

for
i\in[n]
and
k\in[K]
.
This objective is connected to the log likelihood function in the Gaussian mixture model (see Appendix
E.1
for details).
By observing
\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{H}^{\top}

satisfies

\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}\succeq 0,\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=K,\mathbf{Z}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}=\boldsymbol{1}_{n}
, and
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
,
we lift
\mathbf{Z}
into the convex space and solve the following semidefinite problem:

\max_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\langle\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}\rangle,\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z},~\mathbf{Z}\succeq 0,~\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=K,~\mathbf{Z}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}=\boldsymbol{1}_{n},~\text{and}~\mathbf{Z}\geq 0.

(2)

This relaxation, derived in
(
Zhuang et al., 2023
)
, can be solved using off-the-shelf SDP solvers (for example,
(
Sun et al., 2020
)
). Moreover, faster approximation methods for solving this relaxation have been proposed
(
Zhuang et al., 2022
;
Zhuang et al., 2024
)
.

When
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}
is known to be
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
with fixed dimensionality
p
,
Chen and Yang, 2021
proves that this SDP relaxation is a minimax optimal clustering algorithm under the exact recovery loss, with the minimax separation rate for clustering scaling as

\sigma^{2}\big(\log n+\sqrt{(\log n)^{2}+Kp/n}\big)
. This rate increases with
p
at a square root rate. It also highlights that, the SDP relaxation may fail in high-dimensional settings where
p
is comparable or even larger than
n
, unless the separation between clusters also scales with
p
quickly at a square root rate – an assumption that can be very stringent for large
p
.

On the other hand, sparsity assumption has been commonly imposed in the literature to address the challenges related to high dimensionality, and has been verified to hold true in broad application scenarios such as medical imaging
(
Mishra et al., 2017
)
, medical signal processing
(
Zhang et al., 2021
)
, financial modeling
(
Nystrup et al., 2021
)
, and genetics
(
Lee et al., 2015
)
.
This motivates us to investigate how to modify the SDP
K
-means algorithm to incorporate sparsity in high dimensions and explore the corresponding separation rate. Before presenting our algorithm, we first theoretically investigate the fundamental difficulty of the problem and understand the role of sparsity in high-dimensional clustering using SDP relaxation.

2.2
Minimax rate for separation bound in isotropic case

For theoretical study, this section assumes that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}=\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
is known. We study how the separation bound for exact recovery scales with dimension
p
, sample size
n
, and sparsity
s
. We adopt a minimax framework to assess worst-case performance over a distribution class characterized by
\ell_{2}
separation between sparse cluster centers. Given a variable subset
S
, the clustering step of our algorithms presented in Section
3
solves a reduced version of SDP problem (
2
) by restricting to variables in
S
:

\max_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot},\mathbf{Z}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z},~\mathbf{Z}\succeq 0,~\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=K,~\mathbf{Z}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}=\boldsymbol{1}_{n},~\text{and}~\mathbf{Z}\geq 0,

(3)

The analysis proceeds in two steps: First, we show that if there exists a feature subset whose
\ell_{2}
separation (i.e.,
\Delta
in (
4
) below) exceeds a threshold (explicitly determined by the sample size
n
, dimension
p
, and sparsity level
s
) then exact cluster label recovery is possible.
Second, we construct a worst-case distribution where the separation is no more than a constant multiple of this threshold, under which no method can achieve exact recovery. This establishes that no clustering algorithm can succeed under weaker separation conditions.
Together, these results underscore the role of sparsity in problem difficulty and serves as a motivation of our iterative algorithm that incorporates variable selection for addressing sparsity.

We begin by specifying the minimax framework. For each parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K},\mathbf{H})
, where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are cluster centers and
\mathbf{H}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}
denotes the cluster assignment matrix, as explained in (
1
), let
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
be the corresponding joint distribution of
n
independent Gaussian vectors. Each vector has mean given by the assigned cluster center and covariance
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
.
For all
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
we impose two restrictions on
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}
.
First, it has at most
s
nonzero entries. Second, its squared
\ell_{2}
norm exceeds a specified threshold
\Delta^{2}
.
These conditions define the following Gaussian mixture distribution class:

\Theta(s,\Delta,K):=\mathcal{M}(s,\Delta,K)\times\mathcal{H}(K),

(4)

\displaystyle\text{where }\mathcal{M}(s,\Delta,K):=\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\Delta^{2},~\text{for all}~1\leq k\neq l\leq K,

\displaystyle~\cup_{k,l}\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l})=S_{0}\subset[p],|S_{0}|\leq s\bigr\},

and
\mathcal{H}(K):=\bigl\{\mathbf{H}=(H_{i,k})\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}:\sum_{k=1}^{n}H_{i,k}=1~\text{for all }i=1,\ldots,n\bigr\}
.
For a given clustering algorithm
\hat{\mathbf{H}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}(K)
, we assess its performance using the worst-case probability of exact cluster recovery over the distribution class
\Theta(s,\Delta,K)
:

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta(s,\Delta,K)}\min_{\pi\in S_{K}}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\bigl(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H}\bigr),

(5)

where
S_{K}
is the set of label permutations of
[K]
.
For brevity, we omit
\min_{\pi\in S_{K}}
henceforth.

Given a problem instance

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\ast}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast},\mathbf{H}^{\ast})\in\Theta(s,\Delta,K)
,
the true cluster assignment
\mathbf{H}^{\ast}
has one-to-one correspondence to the block-diagonal matrix

\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}:=\mathbf{H}^{\ast}\mathbf{B}^{\ast}(\mathbf{H}^{\ast})^{\top}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}^{\ast}|^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}^{\top},

up to label permutations.
Let us define:

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle:=2\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\left\{\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}+\frac{1}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\biggr)^{-1}\right\},\quad\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}:=\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2},~\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle:=\left\{S:S\subset[p],|S|\leq\sqrt{p},\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim\sigma^{2}\left(\log n+\frac{|S|\log p}{m}+\sqrt{\frac{|S|\log p}{m}}\right)\right\}.

(6)

The following theorem presents a separation condition for exact recovery by the SDP K-means.

Theorem 1

(Separation condition)
.

Assume that there exists a universal constant
C_{1}
such that

m\geq C_{1}n/\log n.

Also assume
|G_{k}^{\ast}|\geq 2
for all
k\in[K]
.
Let
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(S)
be the minimizer of (
3
) corresponding to the subset
S\in\mathcal{S}
. Then

\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(S)=\mathbf{Z}^{*},\forall S\in\mathcal{S})\geq 1-C_{3}K/n,

where
C_{3}>0
is some constant.

The proof of Theorem
1
is presented in Appendix
C
. The stated probability in Theorem
1
holds uniformly over all support sets in
\mathcal{S}
, ensuring the validity of iterative algorithms that solve multiple SDP problems over varying subsets
S
across iterations.
The assumption on
m
prevents overly imbalanced clusters. It is essential for theoretical guarantees for clustering and is either explicitly stated
(
Azizyan et al., 2013
;
Cai et al., 2019
;
Chen and Yang, 2021
;
Chen and Zhang, 2024
;
Jin and Wang, 2016
;
Löffler et al., 2021
;
Verzelen and Arias-Castro, 2017
)
or implicitly acknowledged
(
Giraud and Verzelen, 2019
;
Royer, 2017
)
in much of the literature.

Next, we investigate the fundamental limits of sparse Gaussian mixture clustering. Specifically, we establish that for any clustering method to guarantee worst-case exact recovery over the
\ell_{2}
-separated class in (
4
), its required separation threshold must be at least

\sigma^{2}\log n.

Theorem 2
.

Consider the regime
(s\log p)/n=o(1)
.
For
n\geq 2
, no clustering rule can guarantee exact recovery in the worst case over the class

\overline{\Theta}:=\Theta\bigl(s,C_{4}\sigma^{2}\log n,K\bigr),

with
C_{4}
some small positive constant.
In other words, we have

\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\overline{\Theta}}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H})\geq C_{5},
with
C_{5}
a constant.

The proof of Theorem
2
is presented in Appendix
D
.
Theorem
2
implies that, under the regime of
|S|\log p/m\lesssim\log n
, the separation condition in Theorem
1
(i.e., lower bound for
\Delta^{2}_{S\cap S_{0}}
) is dominated by
\log n
and thus matches the bound in Theorem
2
up to a constant.

The above theoretical results also indicate that, in the sparse regime where
|S_{0}|\ll p
, variable selection can be critical for clustering accuracy. To understand that, consider the simple scenario where
K=2
,
|G_{1}^{\ast}|=|G_{2}^{\ast}|
and
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})_{S_{0}}=\mu_{0}\mathbf{1}_{|S_{0}|}
.
Then Theorems
1
and
2
prove that for any set
S
satisfying

|S|\lesssim\sqrt{p}
,
and

|S|\lesssim(n\log n)/\log p

, exact recovery is possible if and only if

|S\cap S_{0}|\mu_{0}^{2}\gtrsim\log n+\frac{|S|\log p}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{|S|\log p}{n}}\asymp\log n.

This condition becomes very stringent if
S
is a very poor estimate of
S_{0}
(i.e., missing too many signal variables). It also suggests that a slight overestimate of
S_{0}
may not be very damaging. These motivate us to design an algorithm that incorporates some conservative variable selection method into clustering.

3
Proposed iterative algorithms

This section presents our proposed algorithms under two scenarios.
Section
3.1
considers the case where the covariance matrix is known, which includes the setting in Section
2.2
as a special case with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}=\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
. Section
3.2
then extends the methodology to scenarios with unknown covariance. For simplicity, the algorithms are presented for
K=2
, though they naturally extend to any
K
.

3.1
Iterative algorithm under known covariance using the innovated transformation

The success of feature selection critically depends on the signal strength. In the related but different problem of classification, it has been explored in the literature
Fan et al., 2013
;
Fan et al., 2015
that the innovated data transformation of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}
boosts the signal strength for important features the most for a general precision matrix that may not be identity. See
Fan et al., 2013
for detailed arguments and optimality study for the innovated transformation in Gaussian classification.

Our proposed procedure works with the transformed data
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
(or its estimate when
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
unknown, in Section
3.2
).
It alternates between a variable selection step and a clustering step, detailed below, with the complete procedure summarized in Algorithm
2
.

Variable selection step.

As argued in
Cai et al., 2019
, clustering is closely related to classification where the optimal rule is the Bayes rule with linear classification boundary
\boldsymbol{x}^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
for any given test point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Here,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})
. It is seen that the magnitudes of entries in
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
reflect the feature importance. Motivated by this, we select variables by thresholding a rough estimate of the sparse discriminating direction
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
, used solely to guide variable selection without aiming for precise support recovery.
Note that if the true clusters
G_{1}^{*}
and
G_{2}^{*}
were known,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
can be accurately estimated by the sample mean difference
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{1}^{*}}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}^{*}}
, where
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{k}^{*}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
stands for the sample mean vector formed by columns of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
in cluster
k=1,2
.

However, in the clustering problems
G_{1}^{*}
and
G_{2}^{*}
are unknown. We propose to iteratively construct an estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
based on the previous step clustering result. Specifically, at iteration
t
, let the estimated cluster assignments from the previous step be
\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1}
and
\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1}
.
We estimate
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
by the difference of empirical cluster centers:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{t}=\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}},

and form the set of selected features in the current step as

\hat{S}^{t}:=\biggl\{j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:|\hat{\beta}^{t}_{j}|>\sqrt{2\omega_{jj}n\log(2p)}/\sqrt{|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}|\cdot|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}|}\biggr\}.

(7)

We provide some intuition on the above proposed variable selection method. Note that if
j\not\in S_{0}
, then
\tilde{X}_{ji}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mu}_{j},\omega_{jj})
,
where
\omega_{jj}
is the
j
th diagonal entry of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{j}
is the mean value that is invariant to class label.
For any data partition
G_{1}\cup G_{2}=[n]
and
j\not\in S_{0}
, the
j
th entry of the mean difference vector
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{1}}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}}
follows the distribution
\mathcal{N}\bigl(0,(w_{jj}n)/(|G_{1}|\cdot|G_{2}|)\bigr)
. Then we can derive that for any
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\max_{G_{1},G_{2}}\max_{j\in S_{0}^{c}}\left|\sqrt{\frac{|G_{1}|\cdot|G_{2}|}{\omega_{jj}}}(\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{1}}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}})_{j}\right|\leq\sqrt{2n\log\bigl(2(p-|S_{0}|)/\delta\bigr)}\biggr)\geq 1-\delta,

(8)

by the Gaussian maximal inequality (see Appendix
F.1
for details).
We choose a slightly more conservative threshold in (
7
), following the intuition discussed in the last section that some mild over selection does not hurt clustering.

Clustering step.

At iteration
t
, the clustering step solves the reduced version of SDP-relaxed K-means problem (
2
),
formulated with the selected variables
\hat{S}^{t}
and using the identity
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}=\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
.
Since this step is reused in unknown covariance scenario, we present it as Algorithm
1
.
In line 1 of Algorithm
1
,

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}

is different from
(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{X}})_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}
,
and captures the key sparse clustering signal in
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
(see Appendix
E.2
for details).

:

) on matrix

as an affinity matrix

3.2
Iterative algorithm under unknown covariance

We now extend Algorithm
2
to the unknown covariance scenario by adapting the estimation idea proposed in
Fan and Lv, 2016
. The major advantage of our estimation method is that it does not require estimating the full precision matrix
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{*}
. The main idea is that, at the
t
th iteration, we form an estimate of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
directly based on the current cluster assignments without estimating
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{*}
, and then calculate the estimate
\hat{S}^{t}
in (
7
) using the estimated
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
.

The direct estimation of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
is done via the Innovated Scalable Efficient Estimation (ISEE) procedure
(
Fan and Lv, 2016
)
, originally developed for high-dimensional sparse precision matrix estimation. We modify it to selectively estimate only the required quantities for our algorithm, avoiding full precision matrix recovery, and use its theory to set the threshold for variable selection.
Section
3.2.1
summarizes the ISEE procedure adapted as a subroutine in our method, followed by the full algorithm in Section
3.2.2
.

3.2.1
Innovated Scalable Efficient Estimation (ISEE) subroutine

The ISEE procedure is motivated by two key observations. First, the transformed vector
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
remains Gaussian, with

\operatorname{Cov}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i})=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}.

Without loss of generality, assume
p
is an even integer.
Consider a subset
A\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}
of size 2. Assume
i\in G_{1}^{\ast}
, and consider the following decomposition of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A,i}
as the sum of
deterministic mean part and mean-zero random noise vector:

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A,i}=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{A,i},

(9)

Using the the conditional distribution property of multivariate Gaussian and Shur complement, we derive the following regression relationship between sub-vectors of
\mathbf{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})
:

\hskip-16.99998pt\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{A,i}}_{\hskip 9.01396pt\text{response}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}}\hskip-6.99997pt=\hskip 6.99997pt\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}}_{:=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}~\text{(intercept)}}~-~\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}}_{\hskip-21.30568pt\text{slope}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|\times(p-|A|)}}\hskip-16.00008pt\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}}_{\hskip 9.01396pt\text{predictor}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-|A|}}\hskip-13.00005pt+\hskip-15.3pt\underbrace{\mathbf{E}_{A,i}}_{\hskip 17.20844pt\text{residual}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}}\hskip-21.00009pt,

(10)

where
\mathbf{E}_{A,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1})
.
Leveraging this relationship,
the quantities in (
9
) can be further expressed
as follows
(
Fan and Lv, 2016
)
:

(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{A,i}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}\mathbf{E}_{A,i}.

(11)

Appendix
F.2
provides detailed derivations for (
10
) and (
11
). Since
p-|A|
can be comparable or larger than
n
, one may want to leverage the existing regularization method for fitting the regression model (
10
) to obtain sample estimates of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}
,
\mathbf{E}_{A,i}
and
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}
, which further yields plug-in estimate of
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A,i}
. This process can be repeated for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{A}
a partition set of
\{1,\cdots,p\}
consisting of sets of size 2. By combining estimates for
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A,i}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we can form an estimate of the full matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{G_{1}^{*}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{1}^{*}|\times p}
.

A similar expression holds for
i\in G_{2}^{\ast}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}
, respectively. Following the same steps as described above, we can obtain the estimate of the submatrix
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{G_{2}^{*}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{2}^{*}|\times p}
.

Two issues remain to be addressed. First, the true cluster labels are unknown. Second, we need an implementable ISEE subroutine.
Since our algorithm is iterative by nature, we use the cluster assignments in the current iteration as the proxy for true cluster labels to implement the ISEE subroutine, and then iteratively update the estimates for
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
and cluster assignments.
We next explain the ISEE subroutine for a given pair of cluster assignment
(G_{1},G_{2})
.

ISEE subroutine.

The feature indices
[p]
is partitioned into non-overlapping subsets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{L}
, each of size 2 if
p
is even. For odd
p
, we set the size of
A_{L}
to 3. The estimation procedure is then applied for each feature subset, and the results are aggregated. Without loss of generality, we describe the steps for the block
A_{1}
.
Given the previous cluster estimates
\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1}
and
\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1}
, we treat

\{(\mathbf{X}_{A_{1}^{C},i},\mathbf{X}_{A_{1},i}):i\in\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1}\}
as (predictor, response) pair and apply Lasso to estimate the intercept
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{1}}
and residuals

\{\mathbf{E}_{A_{1},i}\}_{i\in\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1}}
.
Similarly, using

\{(\mathbf{X}_{A_{1}^{C},i},\mathbf{X}_{A_{1},i}:i\in\hat{G}_{2}^{t}\}
, we estimate the corresponding
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})_{A_{1}}
and residuals
\{\mathbf{E}_{A_{1},i}\}_{i\in\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1}}
for cluster 2.
We use off-the-shelf tuning techniques to select lasso regularization parameters.
To estimate
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{1},A_{1}}
, we exploit the fact that the residuals
\mathbf{E}_{A_{1},i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{1},A_{1}})^{-1})
, and use the inverse of the estimated sample covariance matrix of
\mathbf{E}_{A_{1},i}
as an estimator. Then applying (
11
), we obtain the estimates for

(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{1}}
,
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{1}}
, and
\{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{A_{1},i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Accordingly, using
(
9
)
, we can estimate
\{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A,i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Repeating this procedure for all blocks
A_{1},\ldots,A_{L}
and stacking the results yields estimates of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}^{\ast}

and
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
.
In summary, we have the output
\texttt{ISEE}(\mathbf{X},\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1},\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1})=(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t},\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t},\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t})
in the
t
th iteration.
The full procedure is detailed in Algorithm
4
in Appendix
F.2
.

3.2.2
Full algorithm with iterative variable selection via ISEE

Given the ISEE subroutine, we extend Algorithm
2
to the unknown covariance scenario. In the variable selection step, we threshold
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t}-\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t}
, instead of
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1}}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1}}
.
In the clustering step, we replace
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}
with the estimated transformed data matrix
\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}
.
We replace
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}
with the pooled sample covariance, combining the sample covariances of

\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}
.
We leverage the convergence rate of the ISEE procedure
(
Fan and Lv, 2016
)
to guide the choice of threshold for
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t}-\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t}
. Specifically, for each
k\in\{1,2\}
and for each block
A_{\ell}
with
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, under some regularity conditions
(
Fan and Lv, 2016
)
, we have:

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-

\displaystyle(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)-\bigl(({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}-({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}\lesssim C_{6}\max\{J\log p/n,\sqrt{\log p/n}\},

(12)

where
J
is the maximum number of nonzero off-diagonal entries per row of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
,
and
C_{6}=\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
.
This bound comes from the convergence rates of
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
and
\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k})_{A_{\ell}}-(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}

proved in
Fan and Lv, 2016
. The detailed derivation is provided in Appendix
F.2
.
In the standard sparse high-dimensional regime of
J\sqrt{\log p/n}=o(1)
, the term
\sqrt{\log p/n}
dominates the bound.
Since ISEE only involves small submatrices of size
2\times 2
or
3\times 3
, the terms in
C_{6}
are bounded and contribute only constant factors. To account for these constants and suppress false discoveries, we include a slowly diverging factor
\sqrt{\log n}
in our threshold.
Combining all the pieces, we set the variable selection threshold as
\sqrt{\log n\log p/n}
.
The final algorithm incorporating these thresholding principles is presented in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Iterative SDP K-means with unknown covariance

1:

Data matrix
\mathbf{X}
, maximum number of iteration
T

2:

\hat{G}_{1}^{0},\hat{G}_{2}^{0}\leftarrow
cluster estimates obtained by running spectral clustering on
\mathbf{X}

\triangleright
Initialization

3:

for
t = 1, 2, …
T

do

4:

\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t},\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t},\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t},\leftarrow\texttt{ISEE}(\mathbf{X},\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1},\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1})
\triangleright
Estimated transformation (Algorithm
4
)

5:

\hat{S}^{t}\leftarrow\left\{j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:|(\widehat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t}-\widehat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t})_{j}|>\sqrt{\log n\log p/n}\right\}

\triangleright
Variable selection

6:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\leftarrow

pooled covariance of covariances computed from
\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}

7:

\hat{G}_{1}^{t},\hat{G}_{2}^{t}\leftarrow\texttt{SDPkmeans}(\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}})

\triangleright
Clustering (Algorithm
1
)

8:

Output
\hat{G}^{T}_{1},\hat{G}^{T}_{2}

4
Numerical results

For all simulations, we consider a symmetric bicluster Gaussian mixture model with equal cluster sizes and a common covariance. For data dimension
p
and sample size
n
, the data are generated as

\mathbf{X}_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}),~i=1,\dots,n/2,~\text{and}~\mathbf{X}_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}),i=n/2+1,\dots,n.

We define
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}
here and set its support to
S_{0}=[10]
. All non-zero entries of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
have the same magnitude, chosen to keep the signal strength, measured by the Mahalanobis distance

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}}=2\sqrt{(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}}=\sqrt{(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}},

as constant, while increasing the noise level through higher ambient dimension
p
.
We evaluate performance using the mis-clustering rate, averaged over 200 independent replications. For initialization, we use spectral clustering
(
Han et al., 2023
)
. We set the number of iteration as
T=100
, but used early stopping criterion. The details on this criterion, code implementation, and details required for replicating our results are provided in Appendix
G.1
.

,

,

Scenario 1: known isotropic covariance

We evaluate Algorithm
2
under the setting of line 1 of Table
1
.
We first compare non-sparse clustering methods, including spectral clustering
(
Han et al., 2023
)
, hierarchical clustering, and SDP-relaxed K-means with all features
(
Chen and Yang, 2021
)
, with Algorithm
2
initialized by each of them. Here, initialization refers to a rough cluster assignment
\hat{G}_{1}^{0},\hat{G}_{2}^{0}
used to guide the first variable selection step.
Results in Figures
1
-(a) and
1
-(b) show that while the non-sparse methods fail to adapt to sparsity, Algorithm
2
, when properly initialized, is robust to high-dimensional noise and effectively adapts to sparsity.
Even moderately good initializations are sufficient, as evidenced by the improved performance of Algorithm
2
when initialized with spectral or SDP K-means clustering. In contrast, poor initializations can hinder recovery, as reflected in the weaker performance of Algorithm
2
initialized by hierarchical clustering.
Next, we benchmark our method against existing sparse clustering approaches, including a two-step method (IF-PCA;
Jin and Wang, 2016
) and iterative methods such as SAS
(
Arias-Castro and Pu, 2017
)
, Sparse K-means
(
Witten and Tibshirani, 2010
)
, and CHIME
(
Cai et al., 2019
)
.
The results in Figures
1
-(c) and
1
-(d) highlight the competitiveness of our approach.
Details of the baseline methods are provided in Appendix
G.2
.

compared to sparse and non-sparse baselines. The dotted lines (non-sparse methods) can also be interpreted as the mis-clustering rates of Algorithm
2
immediately after initialization, before any iteration. The settings are provided in line 1 of Table
1
.

Scenario 2: Unknown covariance.

We evaluate the performance of Algorithm
3
in the setting with a chain-graph sparse precision matrix with conditional correlation
\rho\in\{0.2,0.4\}
, following the setup described in line 2 of Table
1
. Figures
2
-(a) to
2
-(d) compare Algorithm
3
with two alternative variants for the variable selection step: Algorithm
5
estimates the full precision matrix using graphical lasso
(
Friedman et al., 2008
)
, while Algorithm
6
applies thresholding to the empirical cluster means of the transformed data, using a cutoff derived from the Gaussian maximal inequality.
Details of these algorithms are provided in Appendix
G.2
.
The results show that Algorithm
3
outperforms both alternatives, highlighting the benefits of bypassing full precision matrix estimation and employing convergence-rate-guided thresholding.
We also compare our method against other sparsity-aware clustering approaches.
The result presented in Figures
2
-(e) to
2
-(h) show that our method outperforms the others.

,
compared to its variants (Algorithms
5
and
6
; plots a–d)
and other sparsity-aware baseline methods (plots e-h),
under unknown covariance scenario with varying conditional correlations.
The settings are provided in line 2 of Table
1
.

5
Discussion

We investigated the performance of the SDP relaxation of the K-means problem in the context of high-dimensional sparse clustering. Assuming a known isotropic covariance matrix, we derived the minimax separation bound for exact recovery of cluster labels with sparse subsets of features using SDP K-means. Our results demonstrate the critical role of variable selection in achieving exact recovery of cluster labels. Building on this insight, we proposed an iterative algorithm that integrates variable selection into the SDP relaxation framework. Our method avoids the precise estimation of nuisance parameters such as cluster centers and the covariance matrix (which may not be the identity matrix).
There are two potential directions for future research: i) establish theoretical guarantees that our algorithm can identify a subset with sufficient separation, and ii) extend our minimax framework to the general case of an unknown covariance matrix.

Appendix A
Overview of Appendix

This supplementary material provides the technical proofs deferred in the main text, along with some additional results of interest. The content is organized as follows:

•

B
contains the technical lemmas and definitions used in the proof of Theorem
1
.

•

Appendix
C
provides the proof of the separation bound for exact recovery by semidefinite relaxation of K-means, as presented in Theorem
1
.

•

Appendix
D
proves the minimax lower bound for exact recovery, as presented in Theorem
2
.

•

Appendix
E
provides additional details on the SDP objectives deferred from Sections
2.1
and
3.1
, including their derivation and how they capture the sparse clustering signal.

•

Appendix
F
provides an omitted details for the iterative algorithms, which were deferred from Sections
3.1
and
3.2.1
.

•

Appendix
G
provides additional information on the numerical studies presented in Section
4
.

Appendix B
Technical lemmas and definitions

This section presents technical lemmas and definitions that are used throughout the main proofs, beginning the tail bounds for the chi-square distribution.

Lemma 3

(Lemma 1 of
Laurent and Massart, 2000
)
.

If
Z
has a chi-square distribution with
p
degrees of freedom, then for all
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(Z\geq p+2\sqrt{pt}+2t)\leq e^{-t},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}(Z\leq p-2\sqrt{pt})\leq e^{-t}.

Before presenting the next two lemmas, we formally define the sub-exponential norm.

Definition 4
.

Let
\psi_{1}(x):=e^{x}-1
. The sub-exponential norm of a random variable
X
, denoted by
\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}
, is defined as

\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}:=\inf\left\{\lambda>0:\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{1}\left(\frac{|X|}{\lambda}\right)\right]\leq 1\right\}.

Using this definition, Lemma
5
provides an upper bound on the subexponential norm of a chi-square random variable.

Lemma 5

(Lemma VIII.2 of
Chen and Yang, 2021
)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{q})
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\|\|\mathbf{X}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}-q\|_{\psi_{1}}+\|\langle\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}\rangle\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq C\sqrt{q}.

Next, Lemma
6
provides a maximal inequality for sub-exponential norm.

Lemma 6

(Lemma 2.2.2 of
Van Der Vaart and Wellner, 2023
)
.

For random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
, there exists a constant
C_{\psi_{1}}
such that

\left\|\max_{1\leq i\leq m}X_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq C_{\psi_{1}}\,\log(m+1)\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{1}}.

Next, Lemma
7
provides an upper bound on the
\ell_{2}
operator norm of an
m\times n
random matrix with i.i.d. Gaussian entries.

Lemma 7

(Corollary 5.35 of
Vershynin, 2012
)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
entries. Then, for any
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\sigma(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{2t})\bigr)\leq e^{-t}.

Next, Lemma
8
provides a concentration inequality for empirical processes indexed by an unbounded function class.

Lemma 8

(Theorem 4 of
Adamczak, 2008
)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables taking values in a measurable space
(S,\mathcal{B})
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a countable class of measurable functions
f:S\to\mathbb{R}
. Assume that for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and all
i=1,\ldots,n
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}[f(X_{i})]=0
and

\bigl\|\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|f(X_{i})|\bigr\|_{\psi_{1}}<\infty,

Define

Z:=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})\bigr|,~\gamma^{2}:=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Var}[f(X_{i})],~\text{and}~M_{\psi_{1}}:=\|\max_{i\in[n]}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|f(X_{i})|\|_{\psi_{1}}

Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(Z\geq 2\mathbb{E}[Z]+t\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{3\gamma^{2}}\right)+3\exp\left(-\frac{t}{CM_{\psi_{1}}}\right).

Next, Lemma
9
presents a concentration inequality for the suprema of the Gaussian process.

Lemma 9

(Theorem 5.8 of
Boucheron et al., 2016
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
be an almost surely continuous, centered Gaussian process indexed by a totally bounded set
\mathcal{T}
. Define

\gamma^{2}:=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\operatorname{Var}(X_{t}^{2}),\quad\text{and}\quad Z:=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}X_{t}.

Then, we have
\operatorname{Var}(Z)\leq\gamma^{2}
. Moreover, for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}(Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\geq t)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\right).

The next lemma, Dudley’s integral inequality, bounds the expected supremum of a Gaussian process.

Lemma 10

(Theorem 8.1.3 of
Vershynin, 2018
)
.

Let
(T,d)
be a metric space, and let
N(T,d,\varepsilon)
denote the
\varepsilon
-covering number of
T
with respect to
d
.
Let
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a mean-zero random process on a metric space with sub-Gaussian increments. Then

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}X_{t}\leq CK_{\psi_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log N(T,d,\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon,

where
C
is an absolute constant, and
K_{\psi_{2}}
is the sub-Gaussian parameter.

Finally, Lemma
11
presents
the uniform Hanson-Wright inequality for Gaussian quadratic forms.

Lemma 11

(Lemma VIII.4 of
Chen and Yang, 2021
)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{q})
and
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded class of
q\times q
matrices.
Define the random variable

Z=\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}\biggl(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}]\biggr),

which corresponds to the supremum of a centered random process.
Then, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}(|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\left[-C\min\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}},\frac{t}{\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}\right)\right],

where
\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}}\|(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{A}^{T})\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\right]
.

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
1

The proof follows the strategy of Theorem II.1 of
Chen and Yang, 2021
, with modifications to account for the sparsity assumption.
The proof proceeds in three key steps.

1.

Reformulation into high-probability bound problem (Appendix
C.1
):

For each
S
, we construct three statistics that bound a dual certificate
\lambda^{S}
, which verifies exact cluster recovery by the SDP problem (
3
) corresponding to
S
. A nontrivial gap between these bounds guarantees the existence of such a certificate (Corollary
18
).

2.

First bounds and construction of the dual variable (Appendix
C.2
).

Under the separation and set size conditions imposed by
\mathcal{S}
, we derive uniform high-probability bounds on the two statistics defined in the first step and determine the value of
\lambda^{S}
accordingly (Lemma
21
).

3.

Second bounds and conclusion (Appendix
C.3
).

We establish a uniform high-probability bound on the third statistic and verify that the previously chosen value of
\lambda^{S}
exceeds this bound, thereby confirming the existence of a dual certificate and completing the proof.

For notational simplicity, we adopt the following matrix-based notations of our model
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}
for each
i\in G_{k}^{\ast}
, where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p})
:

Definition 12

(Matrix-based notations)
.

We view the data as a matrix
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
and write

\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{M}^{\ast}+\mathcal{E}
,
where
\mathbf{M}^{\ast}
contains the cluster means column-wise, and
\mathcal{E}
collects the noise vectors. For any support set
S\subset[p]
, we denote the
i
th observation and its noise restricted to
S
by

\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{S,i},

respectively. Given true clusters
G_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,G_{K}^{\ast}
, we define the empirical mean and average noise of cluster
k
on support
S
as

\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}:=\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}\mathbf{X}_{S,i},\quad\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\ast}_{S,k}:=\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}\mathcal{E}_{S,i}.

Before we begin, we summarize the new notations used in the proof of Theorem
1
in Table
2
for the reader’s reference.

.

C.1
Reformulation into high-probability bound problem

The primary goal of this section is to derive Corollary
18
, which converts the optimality condtion into high-probability bounds.
Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set,
and consider
the primal SDP problem (
13
) corresponding to
S
, recalled below:

\max_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\langle\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot},\mathbf{Z}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z},~\mathbf{Z}\succeq 0,~\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=K,~\mathbf{Z}\boldsymbol{1}_{n}=\boldsymbol{1}_{n},~\mathbf{Z}\geq 0.

(13)

Let

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Q}^{S},\lambda^{S},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S},\mathbf{B}^{S})

denote the associated Lagrangian function.
Here, the Lagrange multipliers

\mathbf{Q}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\quad\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{B}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

correspond to the primal constraints

\mathbf{Z}\succeq 0,\quad\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=K,\quad\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{Z}\geq 0,

respectively.
Note that
\mathbf{Q}^{S}\succeq 0
and
\mathbf{B}^{S}\geq 0
.
Based on this formulation, this section proceeds in three main steps to ultimately establish Corollary
18
:

1.

Necessary conditions for dual certificates (Appendix
C.1.1
)
:
We formalize sufficient conditions for the existence of dual certificates (Lemma
13
) and leverage them to derive necessary conditions (Lemma
14
) that guide the construction of dual variables.

2.

Reduction to a single dual variable construction (Appendix
C.1.2
):

Leveraging the necessary conditions, we parameterize the dual variables in terms of
\lambda^{S}
, reducing the construction to a one-dimensional problem (Lemma
15
).

3.

Reformulation into high-probability bound problem (Appendix
C.1.3
):

We derive statistics that bound the dual certificate
\lambda^{S}
from above (Lemma
16
) and below (Lemma
17
). This shows that a nontrivial gap between them guarantees the existence of dual variables satisfying the conditions of Lemma
13
(Corollary
18
).

C.1.1
Necessary conditions for dual certificates

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
). Lemma
13
provides sufficient conditions under which the dual variables
\lambda^{S}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}
, and
\mathbf{B}^{S}
form a valid dual certificate (with
\mathbf{Q}^{S}
implicitly captured by the conditions). In particular, the lemma guarantees that the duality gap vanishes at the true cluster membership matrix

\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}^{\top}.

Lemma 13

(Optimality conditions)
.

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and
consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
).
The matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}
is the unique solution to this problem if there exist dual variables
\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{B}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{C}1)\quad\mathbf{B}^{S}\geq 0;

\displaystyle(\mathrm{C}2)\quad\mathbf{W}^{S}:=\lambda^{S}\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{B}^{S}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}-\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}\succeq 0;

\displaystyle(\mathrm{C}3)\quad\langle\mathbf{W}^{S},\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}\rangle=0;

\displaystyle(\mathrm{C}4)\quad\langle\mathbf{B}^{S},\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}\rangle=0;

\displaystyle(\mathrm{C}5)\quad\mathbf{B}^{S}_{\mathbf{G}_{k}^{\ast},\mathbf{G}_{l}^{*}}>0\quad\text{for all}\;1\leq k\neq l\leq K.

In Lemma
13
, conditions (C1) and (C2) ensure dual feasibility, (C3) and (C4) guarantee a zero duality gap, and (C5) ensures the uniqueness of the solution.
The proof is provided in Appendix
C.4.1
.
Building on these conditions, we now establish necessary conditions for dual certificates.
Leveraging this notation, we state the necessary conditions in the following lemma.

Lemma 14

(Necessary conditions for optimality)
.

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and
consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
).
Suppose dual variables

\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and

\mathbf{B}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

satisfy conditions
(C2)
,
(C3)
, and
(C4)
of Lemma
13
.
Then, for all
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
and
i\in G_{k}^{\ast}
, the following statements hold:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}_{i}

\displaystyle=2\mathbf{X}_{S,i}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\frac{\lambda^{S}}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}\|_{2}^{2},~\text{and}~

(14)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbf{B}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast}G_{l}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\bigr)_{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{2|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\lambda^{S}+\frac{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{2}\bigl(\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,l}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr),~

(15)

where

\bigl(\mathbf{B}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast}G_{l}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\bigr)_{i}
denotes the
i
th entry of the
row-wise sum of

\mathbf{B}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast}G_{l}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|\times|G_{l}^{\ast}|}
, which is the

(k,l)
th off-diagonal block of
\mathbf{B}^{S}
.

The proof of Lemma
14
is provided in Appendix
C.4.2
.

C.1.2
Reduction to a single dual variable construction

For any given
\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R}
, condition (
14
) uniquely determines the corresponding value of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}
, which we denote by
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
. We then turn to the dual variable
\mathbf{B}^{S}
and construct
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
, designed to satisfy condition (
15
). The matrix
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
is structured as a block matrix, with symmetric rank-one matrices as its off-diagonal blocks and zero matrices as its diagonal blocks. These constructions follow the proof of Theorem II.1 in
Chen and Yang, 2021
, with modifications to account for varying support sets.

We now describe the construction of
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
. Fix any value of
\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R}
, and for notational simplicity, we suppress the dependence on
\lambda^{S}
in what follows.
For each
k=1,\ldots,K
, define the diagonal block
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{k}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|\times|G_{k}^{\ast}|}
to be the zero matrix.
Next, consider the off-diagonal blocks. Given
\lambda^{S}
, the data, and the true cluster structure, condition (
15
) uniquely determines the row-wise sum of the
(k,l)
off-diagonal block of the dual certificate. Denote this unique vector by
\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}
.
Then, for each
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
, we define the off-diagonal block
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{l}^{\ast}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|\times|G_{l}^{\ast}|}
as the symmetric rank-one matrix:

\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{l}^{\ast}}:=\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)}}\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}(\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)})^{\top},

(16)

This construction ensures that the row-sum condition in (
15
) is satisfied:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{l}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)}}\bigl\{\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}(\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)})^{\top}\bigr\}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)}}\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}\bigr\{(\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}.

We summarize the construction of
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
and
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
in the following lemma.

Lemma 15

(Parametrized dual certificate candidates)
.

Fix the data matrix
\mathbf{X}
and the true cluster structure
G_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,G_{K}^{\ast}
.
Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
). Fix a value of the dual variable
\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Define
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by setting, for each
k=1,\ldots,K
,

\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S}):=2\mathbf{X}_{S,G_{k}^{\ast}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\frac{\lambda^{S}}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{|G_{k}^{\ast}|},

Next, define
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
as the block matrix:

\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S}):=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{1}^{\ast},G_{2}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})&\cdots&\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{1}^{\ast},G_{K}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})\\
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{2}^{\ast},G_{1}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{2}^{\ast},G_{K}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{K}^{\ast},G_{1}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})&\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{K}^{\ast},G_{2}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})&\cdots&\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix},

where for each
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
, the off-diagonal block
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{l}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S})\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|\times|G_{l}^{\ast}|}
is defined as

\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{k}^{\ast},G_{l}^{\ast}}(\lambda^{S}):=\frac{1}{\mathbf{1}_{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}^{\top}\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)}}\,\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}\,(\mathbf{r}^{(l,k,S)})^{\top},

(17)

with
\mathbf{r}^{(k,l,S)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}
defined componentwise by

r_{i}^{(k,l,S)}:=-\frac{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{2|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\lambda^{S}+\frac{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{2}\bigl(\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,l}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr).

(18)

Finally, we construct

\dot{\mathbf{W}}^{S}(\lambda^{S}):=\lambda^{S}\mathbf{I}_{n}-\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\bigl(\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})\bigr)^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}-\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}.

Constructed
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
and
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
satisfy the conditions in Lemma
14
.

C.1.3
Reformulation into high-probability bound problem

Next, we identify conditions of
\lambda^{S}
, that ensures
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
and
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
satisfy the conditions in Lemma
13
. We begin with conditions (C1), (C3), (C4) and (C5). To this end, we introduce the following data-dependent quantities:

D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X}):=\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,l}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2},

and

U^{S}(\mathbf{X}):=\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}+\frac{1}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\biggr)^{-1}\min_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X})\biggr\}.

(19)

Based on these definitions, we now state an upper bound on
\lambda^{S}
:

Lemma 16

(Upper bound on
\lambda^{S}
)
.

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
).
For any value of
\lambda^{S}
, corresponding
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
,
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
, and
\dot{\mathbf{W}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
satisfy conditions (C3) and (C4) of Lemma
13
.
Moreover, if

\lambda^{S}<U^{S}(\mathbf{X}),

then
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
and
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
also satisfy conditions (C1) and (C5) of Lemma
13
.

Proof of Lemma
16
is provided in Appendix
C.4.3
.

Next we move onto condition (C2) of Lemma
13
. To this end, we define some notation.
We first define a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as:

\Gamma_{K}:=\operatorname{span}\left(\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{*}}:k\in[K]\right)^{\perp},

where
\perp
means orthogonal complement.
For each
\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K}
and support set
S\subset[p]
,
we define a collection of data-dependent random variables.
We begin with the following supremum of noise quadratic form process:

L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X}):=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\hskip-13.00005pt\|\mathcal{E}_{S,\cdot}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2},

(20)

Next, we define two fundamental components representing signal–noise and noise–noise interactions:

\displaystyle I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle:=|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}v_{i}\langle(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}},\;\mathcal{E}_{S\cap S_{0},i}\rangle,~\text{and}

(21)

\displaystyle N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle:=|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{*}}v_{i}\bigl\langle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,k}^{\ast}-\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,l}^{\ast},\;\mathcal{E}_{S,i}\bigr\rangle.

(22)

Note that both definitions are asymmetric with respect to the first two indices.
Using these definitions, we introduce three composite random quantities:

\displaystyle T^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})~:=~

\displaystyle I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\cdot I^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X}),

(23)

\displaystyle T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})~:=~

\displaystyle N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\cdot N^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X}),~\text{and}

(24)

\displaystyle T^{S}_{3,kl}(\mathbf{v})~:=~

\displaystyle N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\cdot I^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})+I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\cdot N^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X}).

(25)

Next, the following quantity represents the sum of all elements of

\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}_{G_{l}^{\ast},G_{k}^{\ast}}
,

(l,k)
th off-diagonal block of
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
:

t_{lk}^{S}(\lambda^{S}):=\sum_{j\in G_{l}^{\ast}}r_{j}^{(l,k,S)}=\sum_{j\in G_{l}^{\ast}}\biggl\{\frac{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}{2}D_{lkj}(\mathbf{X},S)-\frac{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{2|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\lambda^{S}\biggr\}.

(26)

Leveraging all these notations, we finally define:

L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\lambda^{S}):=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\biggl\{\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{1}{t^{S}_{lk}(\lambda^{S})}\biggl(T^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})+T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})+T^{S}_{3,kl}(\mathbf{v})\biggr)\biggr\},

(27)

Using this quantity, Lemma
17
states the lower bound condition on
\lambda_{S}
that guarantees condition (C2) of Lemma
13
is satisfied.

Lemma 17

(Lower bound on
\lambda_{S}
)
.

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
).
If

\lambda^{S}>L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})+L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\lambda^{S}),

then the constructed dual variables
\dot{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
and
\dot{\mathbf{B}}^{S}(\lambda^{S})
satisfy condition (C2) of Lemma
13
.

Proof of Lemma
17
is provided in Appendix
C.4.4
.
Combining Lemmas
16
and
17
, we finally arrive at the following corollary:

Corollary 18

(Problem reformulation)
.

Let
S\subset[p]
be a support set, and consider the corresponding primal SDP problem (
13
).
If
there exists
\lambda^{S}
such that

L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})+L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\lambda^{S})<\lambda^{S}<U^{S}(\mathbf{X}),

then the dual variables constructed with this
\lambda^{S}
via Lemma
15
yield a valid certificate
that satisfies all conditions of Lemma
13
, verifying that
\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}
is the unique optimal point of the primal problem.

C.2
First Bounds and Construction of the Dual Variable

This section is devoted to the derivation of Lemma
21
, which establishes that, under the separation condition, there exists a
\lambda^{S}
that partially satisfies the condition stated in Corollary
18
.
We proceed in
two main steps:

1.

High-probability bounds (Appendix
C.2.1
)

:
We derive
a lower bound for
U^{S}(\mathbf{X})
(Lemma
19
)
and
an upper bound for
L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})
(Lemma
20
), under the separation and set size condition of
\mathcal{S}
.

2.

Specifying the value of the dual variable (Appendix
C.2.2
)
: We set the value of
\lambda^{S}
to lie between the bounds established in the previous step (Lemma
21
).

C.2.1
High-probability Bounds

Recall from
(
19
)
that
U^{S}(\mathbf{X})
is defined as

U^{S}(\mathbf{X})=\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}+\frac{1}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\biggr)^{-1}\min_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X})\biggr\},

where

D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X})=\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,l}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{\ast}_{S,k}-\mathbf{X}_{S,i}\|_{2}^{2}.

Recall from (
6
) that we define
\mathcal{S}
as

\mathcal{S}:=\left\{S:S\subset[p],S\cap S_{0}\neq\emptyset,|S|\leq\sqrt{p},\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim\sigma^{2}\left(\log n+\frac{|S|\log p}{m}+\sqrt{\frac{|S|\log p}{m}}\right)\right\},

where

\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}=\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2},

and

m=\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\biggl(\frac{2|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|+|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\biggr)\geq C_{1}\frac{n}{\log n}.

The following lemma establishes a high-probability lower bound for
D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X})
.

Lemma 19
.

Uniformly across
all
index
(k,l,i)
ranging over all pairs
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
and
i\in G^{*}_{k}
, and
all
S\in\mathcal{S}
,
with probability at least
1-C_{7}/n
, it holds that

D^{S}_{kli}(\mathbf{X})\gtrsim\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}+\frac{1}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\biggr)|S|-R^{S}_{kl}.

where
C_{7}>0
is a constant and

R^{S}_{kl}:=\sqrt{\frac{\xi^{S}}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}}\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}+\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}+\frac{1}{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\biggr)\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+\frac{1}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\zeta^{S},

and
\zeta^{S}:=\log(n^{2}|S|^{2}{\binom{p}{|S|}})
.

The proof of Lemma
19
is presented in Appendix
C.4.5
.

Next, Lemma
20
establishes a high-probability upper bound for
L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})
, where we recall from (
20
):

\displaystyle L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\hskip-13.00005pt\|\mathcal{E}_{S,\cdot}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2},

and
\mathcal{E}_{S,\cdot}
is a submatrix of
\mathcal{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
, whose entries are i.i.d. Gaussian random variables.

Lemma 20
.

Uniformly accross all
S\subset[p]
such that
|S|\leq\sqrt{p}
,
with probability at least
1-C_{8}/n
, where
C_{8}>0
is a constant, it holds that

L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})\lesssim\sigma^{2}(n+\log n+|S|\log p).

The proof of Lemma
20
is provided in Appendix
C.4.6
.

C.2.2
Specifying the Value of the Dual Variable

Since
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\lambda^{S})
in (
27
) depends on
\lambda^{S}
, we first specify a value which lies between
L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})
and
U^{S}(\mathbf{X})
with high probability,
uniformly over
S\in\mathcal{S}
.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\dot{\lambda}^{S}:=\sigma^{2}|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}/4
.
Then Uniformly over
S\in\mathcal{S}
, with probability at least
1-C_{9}/n
, where
C_{9}>0
is a constant, it holds that

L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})\lesssim\dot{\lambda}^{S}\lesssim U^{S}(\mathbf{X}).

Proof of Lemma
21
is provided in Appendix
C.4.7
.

C.3
Second high-probability bounds and conclusion

This section completes the proof by showing that, with high probability,

L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})\lesssim\dot{\lambda}^{S}
.
As defined in equation (
27
),
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})
is the supremum of an empirical process involving statistics

t^{S}_{lk}
(
26
),

I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

I^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
(
21
),

N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
,
and

N^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
(
22
).
Therefore, the bound on
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})
follows directly from bounds on the
t^{S}_{lk}
,
I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
and
N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
. Accordingly, the remainder of this section proceeds as follows:

1.

Bounding the off-diagonal block sum of the dual variable matrix (Appendix
C.3.1
)
: We derive a high-probabilty lower bound for
t^{S}_{lk}
(Lemma
22
).

2.

Bounding the signal–noise interaction term (Appendix
C.3.2
):

We derive a high-probability upper bound for
I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

(Lemma
23
)
and show that the corresponding component of
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})
is dominated by
\dot{\lambda}^{S}
(Lemma
24
).

3.

Bounding the noise–noise interaction term (Appendix
C.3.3
):

We derive a high-probability upper bound for
N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

(Lemmas
25
and
26
),
and show that
the corresponding component of
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})
is dominated by
\dot{\lambda}^{S}
(Lemma
27
).

4.

Conclusion (Appendix
C.3.4
)
.

C.3.1
Bounding the off-diagonal block sum of the dual variable matrix

With the value of
\dot{\lambda}^{S}

set in Lemma
21
, we provide a high-probability lower bound of
t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})
defined in (
26
).

Lemma 22
.

For any
1\leq l\neq k\leq K
, if
|G_{l}^{\ast}|\geq 2
and
|G_{k}^{\ast}|\geq 2
,
uniformly over
S\in\mathcal{S}
, with probability at least
1-C_{7}/n
, where
C_{0}
is the constant used in Lemma
19
, it holds that

t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})\gtrsim|G_{l}^{\ast}|\;|G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr).

The proof of Lemma
22
is provided in Appendix
C.4.8
.

C.3.2
Bounding the signal-noise interaction term

We first provide a high-probability upper bound of

I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})=|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{\ast}}v_{i}\langle(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}},\;\mathcal{E}_{S\cap S_{0},i}\rangle,

defined in (
21
).

Lemma 23
.

Uniformly across
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{10}/n
, where
C_{10}>0
is a constant, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim|G_{l}^{\ast}|\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{l}^{\ast})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}\biggl(|G_{k}^{\ast}|+\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}|\log n}+\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}||S|\log p}+\log n+|S|\log p\biggr)^{1/2}.

The proof of Lemma
23
is provided in Appendix
C.4.9
. Building on this result, we establish the first partial upper bound for
L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})
, corresponding to the term

T^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})=I^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\cdot I^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
, defined in (
23
).

Lemma 24
.

Uniformly across
S\in\mathcal{S}
,
with probability at least
1-C_{11}/n
, where
C_{11}>0
is a constant, it holds that

\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{T^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})}{t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})}\lesssim\dot{\lambda}^{S}.

Proof of Lemma
24
is provided in Appendix
C.4.10
.

C.3.3
Bounding the noise-noise interaction term

Since
N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})
, defined in (
22
), is an inner product of dependent Gaussian variables, we decompose it into a sum of inner products between independent Gaussian variables.

\displaystyle N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{*}}v_{i}\bigl\langle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,k}^{\ast}-\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,l}^{\ast},\;\mathcal{E}_{S,i}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{*}}v_{i}\bigl\langle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,k}^{\ast},\;\mathcal{E}_{S,i}\bigr\rangle-|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{*}}v_{i}\bigl\langle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,l}^{\ast},\;\mathcal{E}_{S,i}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\sum_{\{(i,j)\in G_{k}^{\ast}\}}\hskip-15.00002ptv_{i}\left\langle\mathcal{E}_{S,i},\mathcal{E}_{S,j}\right\rangle}_{:=G^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})}\underbrace{-|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sum_{i\in G_{k}^{*}}v_{i}\bigl\langle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S,l}^{\ast},\;\mathcal{E}_{S,i}\bigr\rangle,}_{:=G^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})}

We now present high-probability upper bounds for
G^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})
and
G^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})
in order. Since all terms consist purely of noise random variables, no separation condition is needed in this case.

Lemma 25
.

Uniformly across
S\in[p]
such that
|S|\leq\sqrt{p}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{11}/n
,
where
C_{11}>0
is a constant,
it holds that

\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\hskip-10.00002ptG^{S}_{1,kl}(\mathbf{v})\lesssim|G_{l}^{\ast}|\sqrt{|S|(\log n+|S|\log p)}.

The proof of Lemma
25
is provided in Appendix
C.4.11
.

Lemma 26
.

Uniformly across
S\in[p]
such that
|S|\leq\sqrt{p}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{12}/n
, where
C_{12}>0
is a constant, it holds that

\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}|G_{2,kl}^{S}(\mathbf{v})|\lesssim\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\sqrt{|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}}\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+\sqrt{\xi^{S}}},

where
\xi^{S}=\log(n|S|^{2}{\binom{p}{|S|}})
.

The proof of Lemma
26
is provided in Appendix
C.4.12
.

Lemma 27
.

Uniformly across
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{13}/n
, where
C_{13}>0
is a constant, it holds that

\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})}{t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})}\lesssim\dot{\lambda}^{S}.

Proof of Lemma
27
is provided in Appendix
C.4.13
.

C.3.4
Conclusion

The inequality regarding
T^{S}_{3,kl}(\mathbf{v})
is a direct consequence of Lemmas
24
, and
27
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Therefore, by combining Lemmas
21
,
24
, and
27
, we conclude that, uniformly over
S\in\mathcal{S}
, with probability at least
1-C_{14}/n
, where
C_{14}>0
is a constant, the quantity
\dot{\lambda}^{S}
constructed in Lemma
20
satisfies

L_{1}^{S}(\mathbf{X})+L_{2}^{S}(\mathbf{X},\dot{\lambda}^{S})\lesssim\dot{\lambda}^{S}\lesssim U^{S}(\mathbf{X}).

Therefore, by Corollary
18
, the dual variables constructed in Lemma
15
serve as dual certificates verifying that
\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}
is the unique optimal solution to the primal SDP problem corresponding to
S
, stated in (
13
).
This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

C.4
Proof of Supporting Lemmas

This section presents omitted proof of lemmas presented in the proof of Theorem
1
.

C.4.1
Proof of Lemma
13

The proof proceeds in three steps:

1.

Conditions (C1) and (C2) from dual feasibility,

2.

Conditions (C3) and (C4) from zero dual gap,

3.

Condition (C5) from the block-diagonal structure of
\mathbf{Z}^{\ast}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}^{\ast}|^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{k}^{\ast}}^{\top}
.

Proof.

We proceed by following the steps outlined above in sequence.

1. Conditions (C1) and (C2) from dual feasibility.

After simplification, the Lagrangian function becomes an affine function of
\mathbf{Z}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Q}^{S},\lambda^{S},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S},\mathbf{B}^{S})

\displaystyle\quad\quad:=\langle\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot},\;\mathbf{Z}\rangle+\langle\mathbf{Q}^{S},\mathbf{Z}\rangle+\lambda^{S}(K-\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{Z}))+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\biggl(\mathbf{1}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{Z}+\mathbf{Z}^{\top})\mathbf{1}_{n}\biggr)+\langle\mathbf{B}^{S},\mathbf{Z}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\lambda^{S}K+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n}+\bigl\langle\mathbf{Q}^{S}-\mathbf{W}^{S},\;\mathbf{Z}\bigr\rangle,

where
we use
\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{Z})=\langle\mathbf{I}_{n},\mathbf{Z}\rangle
and
recall from Lemma
13
that

\mathbf{W}^{S}=\lambda^{S}\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{B}^{S}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}-\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}.

Therefore, the Lagrange dual function, shown below, can be derived analytically, as a linear function is bounded only when the slope part is zero.

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{Q}^{S},\lambda^{S},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S},\mathbf{B}^{S})

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Q}^{S},\lambda^{S},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S},\mathbf{B}^{S})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{S}K+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n}+\sup_{\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\bigl\langle\mathbf{Q}^{S}-\mathbf{W}^{S},\;\mathbf{Z}\bigr\rangle,

As a result, we have:

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{Q}^{S},\lambda^{S},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S},\mathbf{B}^{S})=\begin{cases}\lambda^{S}K+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n},\quad&\mathbf{Q}^{S}=\mathbf{W}^{S}~(\text{slope is }\mathbf{0})\\
\infty,\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Making the following domain of the dual function
as an explicit linear equality constraint, and combining it with

\mathbf{Q}^{S}\succeq 0
and
\mathbf{B}^{S}\geq 0
, with some abuse of terminology,
we refer to the following problem as the Lagrange dual problem associated with the primal SDP problem

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda^{S}\in\mathbb{R},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbf{B}^{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\hskip-20.00003pt\lambda^{S}K+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n},\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{B}^{S}\geq 0,\mathbf{W}^{S}\succeq 0.

The constraints in this optimization problem correspond to conditions (C1) and (C2).

2. Conditions (C3) and (C4) from zero dual gap.

The duality gap is simply the difference between the dual and primal objective function:

\displaystyle\lambda^{S}K+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{S})^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n}-\langle\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{S,\cdot},\;\mathbf{Z}\rangle
