\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})}{t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\frac{N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\;N^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})}{t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\lesssim}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\frac{N^{S}_{kl}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})\;N^{S}_{lk}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{X})}{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\frac{\bigl(G_{1,kl}^{S}(\mathbf{v})+G_{2,kl}^{S}(\mathbf{v})\bigr)\bigl(G_{1,lk}^{S}(\mathbf{v})+G_{2,lk}^{S}(\mathbf{v})\bigr)}{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

where step
(i)
uses the definition of
T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})
stated in (
24
)
and step
(ii)
uses Lemma
22
.
Expanding the numerator yields four terms. We establish the inequality for two of them, the
G_{1}\times G_{1}
and
G_{2}\times G_{2}
terms, as the cross terms can be easily handled by leveraging the results from these two cases. Before proceeding, we recall from (
6
) that
\mathcal{S}
is defined as

\mathcal{S}=\left\{S:S\subset[p],|S|\leq\sqrt{p},\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim\left(\log n+\frac{|S|\log p}{m}+\sqrt{\frac{|S|\log p}{m}}\right)\right\}.

2. Inequality for the
G_{1}\times G_{1}
term.

By Lemma
25
, Uniformly across
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{11}/n
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\frac{G_{1,kl}^{S}(\mathbf{v})G_{1,lk}^{S}(\mathbf{v})}{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\lesssim}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{|S|(\log n+|S|\log p)}{\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|S|\log n+|S|^{2}\log p}{\bigl(\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}.

Therefore it suffices to show that

|S|\log n+|S|^{2}\log p\lesssim\bigl(|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\bigr)\bigl(\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|\bigr),

which is true
since
|S|\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim|S|\log n
and
m|S|\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}\gtrsim|S|^{2}\log p
under the separtion condition of
\mathcal{S}
.

3. Inequality for the
G_{2}\times G_{2}
term.

By Lemma
26
, Uniformly across
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
(k,l)
such that
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
,
with probability at least
1-C_{12}/n
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\frac{G_{2,kl}^{S}(\mathbf{v})G_{2,lk}^{S}(\mathbf{v})}{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\lesssim}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\biggl\{\frac{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\sqrt{|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}}}{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+\sqrt{\xi^{S}}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\times\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}|}\sqrt{|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}}\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}|+\sqrt{\xi^{S}}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}||G_{l}^{\ast}|+\sqrt{\xi^{S}}\bigl(|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|\bigr)+\xi^{S}}}{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\bigl(\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}||G_{l}^{\ast}|}+\sqrt{\sqrt{\xi^{S}}\bigl(|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|\bigr)}+\sqrt{\xi^{S}}\bigr)}{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)},

where

\xi^{S}=\log(n|S|^{2}{\binom{p}{|S|}})
.
The last term can be decomposed into three parts, and we establish an inequality for each one in turn, starting with the first term:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\bigl(\sqrt{|G_{k}^{\ast}||G_{l}^{\ast}|}\bigr)}{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)}{\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\bigl(|S|+|S|\sqrt{\log p}+\sqrt{|S|\log n}+\log n+|S|\log p\bigr)}{\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|}

\displaystyle\lesssim|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2},

where the last inequality holds due to the sepration condition of
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, for the second term,
we first recall from Theroem
1
that

m=2\min_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\left\{\frac{|G_{k}^{\ast}||G_{l}^{\ast}|}{|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|}\right\}.

Then we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\bigl(\sqrt{\sqrt{\xi^{S}}\bigl(|G_{k}^{\ast}|+|G_{l}^{\ast}|\bigr)}\bigr)}{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)},

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)(\xi^{S})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{m}\bigl(\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2},

where the last inequality holds because under the separation condition of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\lesssim|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}
and
(\xi^{S})^{1/4}\lesssim\sqrt{m}\bigl(\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|\bigr)
.

Finally, for the third term, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\sqrt{\xi^{S}}}{\sqrt{|G_{l}^{\ast}||G_{k}^{\ast}|}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\sqrt{\xi^{S}}}{\sqrt{m}\bigl(\|(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{l})_{S\cap S_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}+|S|\bigr)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2},

where the last inequality holds because under the separation condition of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\bigl(|S|+2\sqrt{|S|\xi^{S}}+2\xi^{S}\bigr)\lesssim|S|+m\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}
and
(\xi^{S})^{1/2}\lesssim\sqrt{m}\bigl(\Delta_{S\cap S_{0}}^{2}+|S|\bigr)
.

4. Conclusion.

The inequalities for the cross terms can be verified in a straightforward manner. Combining all the inequalities established above, we conclude that, uniformly over
S\in\mathcal{S}
and all
(k,l)
with
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
, the following holds with probability at least
1-C_{13}/n
, where
C_{13}>0
is a constant, it holds that:

\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\Gamma_{K},\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{1\leq k\neq l\leq K}\frac{T^{S}_{2,kl}(\mathbf{v})}{t^{S}_{lk}(\dot{\lambda}^{S})}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
27
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
2

The proof proceeds in the following intermediate steps:

1.

Reduction to symmetric two-cluster problem (
64
),

2.

Reduction to average case risk (
65
),

3.

Reduction to Bayes risk (
66
),

4.

Bounding the Bayes risk (
68
).

Proof.

We proceed by following the steps outlined above in sequence.

1. Reduction to symmetric two-cluster problem.

We denote the separation by
\rho>0
and leave its value unspecified for now.
First, we define a reduced class of distributions consisting of two symmetric, sparse cluster centers.

\tilde{\Theta}(s,\rho,2):=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)\times\mathcal{H}(2)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2):=\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=-\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\rho^{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=S_{0}\subset[p],|S_{0}|\leq s\bigr\},

Then we have (by augmenting the matrices in
\mathcal{H}(2)
with zero columns to match the dimensions):

\tilde{\Theta}(s,\rho,2)\subset\Theta(s,\rho,K).

This inclusion immediately implies that

\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta(s,\rho,K)}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H})\geq\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}(s,\rho,2)}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H}),

(64)

since the supremum over a smaller set is less than or equal to that over a larger set.

2. Reduction to average case risk.

Next, we lower bound the worst-case risk in (
64
) by the average risk.

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}(s,\rho,2)}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)}\sup_{\mathbf{H}\in\mathcal{H}(2)}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{X})\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}\neq\mathbf{H}),

(65)

where
\pi
is a uniform prior on
\mathcal{H}(2)
, formally given by,
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,

H_{i,1}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{Ber}(1/2),\quad H_{i2}=1-H_{i1}.

3. Reduction to Bayes risk.

The risk (
65
) is equivalent to the minimax risk under zero-one loss, where the data is generated from a uniform hierarchical Gaussian mixture model defined as follows:

\displaystyle H_{i,1}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1/2),

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{i}\mid H_{i,1}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\left((-1)^{H_{i,1}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\;,\mathbf{I}_{p}\right).

In this setting, the algorithm that minimizes

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\bigl(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H}\bigr)

is the Bayes classifier
\mathbf{H}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,
which assumes knowledge of the true cluster centers. It operates by thresholding the Gaussian likelihood ratio, and under symmetric cluster centers with isotropic covariance, this yields the decision rule

H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{i,1}=\mathbbm{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}>0\right)
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Its error probability for one observation, known as the Bayes rate, is explicitly given by
(
Cai and Zhang, 2019
)
:

\mathcal{R}^{\ast}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}:=\Phi\biggl(-\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}}{2\sigma}\biggr),

(66)

where
\Phi
denotes the cumulative distribution function of standard normal distribution.
Therefore we can bound (
65
) further from below:

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{X})\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}\neq\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle~\overset{(i)}{\geq}\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)}\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{X})\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}\neq\mathbf{H})

\displaystyle~\overset{(ii)}{=}\sup_{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(s,\rho,2)}1-(1-\mathcal{R}^{\ast}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}})^{n}

\displaystyle~\overset{(iii)}{=}1-\bigl(1-\Phi(-\rho/\sigma)\bigr)^{n},

(67)

where step
(i)
applies the max–min inequality (see, for example, Section 5.4.1 of Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004),
step
(ii)
follows from the optimality of Bayes classifier, and
step
(iii)
uses (
66
).

4. Bounding the Bayes risk.

If we set
\rho^{2}=C_{4}\sigma^{2}\log n
, we have

\Phi(-\rho/\sigma)=\Phi\bigl(-\sqrt{C_{4}\log n}\bigr)=1-\mathbb{P}(Z>-\sqrt{C_{4}\log n})\overset{(i)}{\geq}1-\exp(-\frac{C_{4}}{2}\log n)=1-n^{-C_{4}/2},

where
Z
denotes a standard normal random variable and step
(i)
uses Gaussian tail bound.
Leveraging this, for any
n\geq 2
, we further bound (
67
) as:

1-\bigl\{1-\Phi\bigl(-\sqrt{C_{4}\log n}\bigr)\bigr\}^{n}\geq 1-n^{-C_{4}n/2}>C_{5},

(68)

where
C_{5}>0
depends on
C_{4}
.
Collecting the reductions (
64
), (
65
), (
66
), and the bound (
68
), we conclude that

\inf_{\hat{\mathbf{H}}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta(s,\sigma^{2}\log n,K)}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{X})\neq\mathbf{H})>C_{5}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
2
.
∎

Remark 29
.

The reduction in step
(i)
of (
67
) is not tight in some regime. A sharper analysis can be obtained by decomposing the average risk

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{X})\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}\neq\mathbf{H})

into the Bayes risk
\mathcal{R}^{\ast}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}
and the excess risk

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{X})\sim\pi}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\hat{\mathbf{H}}\neq\mathbf{H})-\mathcal{R}^{\ast}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}
.

Cai et al., 2019
shows that the excess risk is of order
(s\log p)/n
in a setting slightly different from ours. Rather than modifying their analysis, we assume a standard high-dimensional sparse regime
s\log p/n=o(1)
, so that the Bayes risk term
\log n
dominates. In this regime, this lower bound matches the separation bound established in Theorem
1
.

Appendix E
Details for the SDP objective function

This section presents supplementary information for the original SDP objective (Appendix
E.1
) and the reduced SDP objective in Algorithm
1
(Appendix
E.2
).

E.1
Derivation of the SDP objective function

This section presents the derivation of the SDP objective (
1
), which was omitted from Section
2.1
for brevity.
The derivation follows Sections 2 and 3 of
Zhuang et al., 2023
and Section 1 of
Chen and Yang, 2021
, and is included here for completeness.
We proceed in the following order:

1.

From Gaussian mixture likelihood to K-means objective,

2.

From K-means objective to SDP objective.

1. From Gaussian mixture likelihood to K-means objective.

We assume that the common covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}
is known.
The log-likelihood for the parameters
G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}
is

\ell(G_{1},\ldots,G_{K},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K})=-\frac{np}{2}\log(2\pi|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}|)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}),

where

|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}|
denotes the determinant of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}
,

G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup\ldots\cup G_{K}=[n]

and

G_{k}\cap G_{l}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq k\neq l\leq K
.
Since the cluster center
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
’s are not of direct interest, we substitute each
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
with its maximum likelihood estimate given the clustering
G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}
:

\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{k}:=\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{j\in G_{k}}\mathbf{X}_{j}.

By further omitting constants that are irrelevant to the clustering assignment
G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}
, and multiplying the remaining term by 2,
we define the scaled profile log-likelihood for the clustering as follows:

\ell_{\mathrm{lik}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{K};\mathbf{X}):=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{k})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{k}).

(69)

The following is a well-known identity, which can be found in Equation 12.18 of
James et al., 2013
:

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{lik}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{K};\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{k})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{k})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{j\in G_{k}}\mathbf{X}_{j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{j\in G_{k}}\mathbf{X}_{j})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j}).

(70)

If
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, the maximization of (
70
) is formulated as the following K-means problem, by removing the constant factor
-1/2
:

\displaystyle\min_{G_{1},\ldots,G_{K}}

\displaystyle~\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}\|\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}

(71)

\displaystyle s.t.

\displaystyle~G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup\ldots\cup G_{K}=\{1,\ldots,n\},~\text{and}

\displaystyle~G_{k}\cap G_{l}=\emptyset~\text{for all}~1\leq k\neq\leq K,

where within-cluster dissimilarity is quantified by the sum of squared Euclidean distances.

2. From K-means objective to SDP objective.

The likelihood objective (
70
) can be further simplified as

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{lik}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{K};\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}\bigl(2\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{j}-\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{j}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\biggl(\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{j}-\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i\in G_{k}}|G_{k}|(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i})\biggr)

\displaystyle=\ell_{\operatorname{sdp}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i},

(72)

where we recall that in (
1
) and (
2
), the SDP objective function is defined as follows:

\ell_{\operatorname{sdp}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k};\mathbf{X}):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i}.

(73)

From (
72
), we have the following relationship between the likelihood objective (
69
) and the SDP objective (
73
):

\displaystyle\ell_{\operatorname{sdp}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k};\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=\ell_{\operatorname{lik}}(G_{1},\ldots,G_{k};\mathbf{X})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}_{i}.

The second term of the SDP objective does not depend on the cluster structure.

E.2
Intuition for the SDP objective in Algorithm
1

This section provides intuition on how the objective

\langle\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot},\mathbf{Z}\rangle

in Line 1 of Algorithm
1

arises from the original SDP objective

\langle\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}\rangle
in (
2
),
when restricted to a support set
S
, while capturing the key sparse clustering signal

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})
.

We temporarily assume that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
is known, and denote the transformed data matrix as
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
. Under this transformation, the similarity matrix in the original SDP objective (
2
) becomes

\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathbf{X}=\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}.

We use the matrix-based notation introduced in Definition
12
.
Here,

\mathbf{M}^{\ast}
denotes the deterministic mean matrix, where each column is either
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}
or
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast}
,
and, the data matrix and its transformed version can be written as:

\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{M}^{\ast}+\mathcal{E}~\text{and}~\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}+\tilde{\mathcal{E}},~\text{where}~\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}~\text{and}~\tilde{\mathcal{E}}:=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\mathcal{E},

and the columns of
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{n})
are i.i.d. random vectors distributed as
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})
.
Using this notation, the similarity matrix can be decomposed into:

\mathbf{A}=(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}+(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}+\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}+\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}.

In the above decomposition, both the second and third terms are zero-mean, and
the fourth term is independent of the cluster structure.
Thus we focus on
(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
.

Recall that
S_{0}\subset[p]
denotes the index set where the corresponding entries of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{2}
differ (signal).
Let
N:=[p]\setminus S_{0}
denote the index set where the corresponding entries of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{2}
are identical (noise).
Since
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})
is sparse,
the row indices of
\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
can also be decomposed into the signal set
S_{0}
and the noise set
N
.
Then
(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}
is decomposed into

\displaystyle(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot}+(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},N}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{N,\cdot}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{N,\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{N,S_{0}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot}+(\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{N,\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{N,N}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{N,\cdot}.

In the above decomposition, the first term,
(\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot}
contains all the essential information for clustering, by the following reason.
By expressing
\mathbf{Z}
as
\mathbf{HBH}
and tracing back the derivation in Appendix
E.1
, we have:

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle(\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot}\mathbf{Z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}{\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\sum_{i,j\in G_{k}}(\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}-\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}(\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}-\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},j})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},i}.

The first term contains
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
.
Therefore, we propose running the SDP-relaxed K-means (
2
) using a noisy version of
(\mathbf{M}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S_{0},S_{0}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\ast}_{S_{0},\cdot}
, given by the matrix

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{\ast}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot}.

Appendix F
Details for Iterative Algorithms

This section supplements the iterative algorithms presented in Section
3
by providing omitted details. Appendix
F.1
derives the uniform bound presented in (
8
), which is used in the variable selection step of Algorithm
2
. Appendix
F.2
provides additional details for the ISEE subroutine, which plays a central role in Algorithm
3
.

F.1
Derivation of the uniform bound in the variable selection step of Algorithm
2

This section derives the uniform bound presented in equation (
8
) in Section
3.1
.
Since
\mathbf{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})
, it follows that

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}).

Therefore, for any
j\notin S_{0}
, we have
\tilde{X}_{j,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\omega_{jj})
, where
\omega_{jj}
is the
j
th diagonal entry of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
.

Given cluster assignments
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, the empirical transformed cluster means are defined as

\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{1}}:=\frac{1}{|G_{1}|}\sum_{i\in G_{1}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i},\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}}:=\frac{1}{|G_{2}|}\sum_{i\in G_{2}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{i}.

Regardless of the accuracy of the cluster assignments, for
j\notin S_{0}
, the
j
th entry of
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{1}}
and
\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}}
follows the distributions

\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\omega_{jj}}{|G_{1}|}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\omega_{jj}}{|G_{2}|}\right),

respectively. Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(G_{1},G_{2}):=\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G}-\bar{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{G_{2}}
. Then, for each
j\notin S_{0}
, the scaled
j
th element satisfies:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{jj}}}\hat{\beta}_{j}(G_{1},G_{2})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{n}{|G_{1}|\cdot|G_{2}|}\right),

because
|G_{1}|+|G_{2}|=n
.
Although the entries of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(G_{1},G_{2})
are not independent, the Gaussian maximal inequality still applies. Consequently, for any
\delta>0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have:

\max_{G_{1},G_{2}}\max_{j\in S\setminus S_{0}}\left|\sqrt{\frac{|G_{1}|\cdot|G_{2}|}{\omega_{jj}}}\hat{\beta}_{j}(G_{1},G_{2})\right|\leq\sqrt{2n\log\left(2(p-|S_{0}|)/\delta\right)}.

This completes the derivation of the bound in equation (
8
).

F.2
Supplememtary details for ISEE subroutine

This section provides
the omitted details for the ISEE subroutine. We proceed in the following steps:

1.

Regression relationship on the data sub-vectors (Appendix
F.2.1
),

2.

Key identities on transformed cluster center and noise (Appendix
F.2.2
),

3.

Estimation error bound for the ISEE subroutine (Appendix
F.2.3
),

Without loss of generality, we focus on the case where
i\in G_{1}^{\ast}
.
The derivations build on those in
Fan and Lv, 2016
, with the key distinction that they only consider the special case
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
At the end of this section,
Algorithm
4
formulates the ISEE subroutine presented in Section
3.2.1
as an algorithm.

F.2.1
Regression relationship on the data sub-vectors

Using the conditional distribution property of multivariate Gaussians and the Schur complement, we derive the following regression relationship between sub-vectors of
\mathbf{X}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})
for
i\in G_{1}^{\ast}
, as presented in equation (
10
) in Section
3.2.1
.
Given a small index set
A\subset[p]
,
we recall the following relationship:

\hskip-16.99998pt\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{A,i}}_{\hskip 9.01396pt\text{response}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}}\hskip-6.99997pt=\hskip 6.99997pt\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}}_{:=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}~\text{(intercept)}}~-~\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}}_{\hskip-21.30568pt\text{slope}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|\times(p-|A|)}}\hskip-16.00008pt\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}}_{\hskip 9.01396pt\text{predictor}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-|A|}}\hskip-13.00005pt+\hskip-15.3pt\underbrace{\mathbf{E}_{A,i}}_{\hskip 17.20844pt\text{residual}\in\mathbb{R}^{|A|}}\hskip-21.00009pt,

where
\mathbf{E}_{A,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1})
.
Let us partition
\mathbf{X}_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
according to :

\mathbf{X}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{X}_{A,i}\\
\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}\end{bmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}=\begin{bmatrix}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}\\
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}\end{bmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A^{c}}\end{bmatrix}.

Then the conditional distribution
\mathbf{X}_{A,i}\mid\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}
follows
\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, where

\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c}A^{c}})^{-1}\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A^{c},i}-(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}\bigr),

(74)

and

\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A^{c}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A}.

(75)

Next, to apply the Schur complement, we first introduce the following simplfied notation for the matrix partition:

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A^{c}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}\\
\mathbf{C}&\mathbf{D}\end{bmatrix},

(76)

Then by the Schur complement, we have:

\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast})^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}&\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\\
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A}&\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A^{c}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{W}&-\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\\
-\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{W}&\mathbf{D}^{-1}+\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{bmatrix},

(77)

where

\mathbf{W}:=(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C})^{-1}.

(78)

Leveraging this representation of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
, we can derive:

-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{A,A^{c}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{A^{c},A^{c}}^{\ast})^{-1}\overset{(i)}{=}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\overset{(ii)}{=}\mathbf{W}^{-1}(-\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1})\overset{(iii)}{=}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A^{c}}^{\ast},

where
step
(i)
uses (
76
),
step
(ii)
uses (
78
), and
step
(iii)
uses
(
77
).
This lets us rewrite
\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in (
74
) as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c}A^{c}})^{-1}\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A^{c},i}-(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}-(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{AA}^{\ast})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{AA^{c}}^{\ast}\bigl(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A^{c},i}-(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A^{c}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}}_{(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}}-(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A^{c}}^{\ast}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{A^{c},i},

Likewise, we can also simplify
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
in (
75
) as follows:

\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A^{c}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}_{A^{c},A}\overset{(i)}{=}\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}\overset{(ii)}{=}\mathbf{W}^{-1}=(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1},

where
step
(i)
uses (
76
),
step
(ii)
uses (
78
), and
step
(iii)
uses
(
77
).
Substituting these expressions into the conditional distribution

\mathbf{X}_{A,i}\mid\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
,
we obtain the regression relationship (
10
).

F.2.2
Key identities on transformed cluster center and noise

Next, we turn to the two key identities presented in (
11
):

(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A},

and

(\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{i})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}\mathbf{E}_{A,i},

which play a central role in the ISEE subroutine.

We start with the first identity.
By pre-multiplying
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast}
to

(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}
, we have:

\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A,A^{c}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}.

Therefore, we can express
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}
as follows:

(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,\cdot}\;\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A}.

This completes the derivation of the first identity.

Next, we derive the second identity:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{A,i}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,\cdot}\;\mathbf{X}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,\cdot}\;\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}\mathbf{X}_{A,i}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast})_{A^{c}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}\biggl(\mathbf{X}_{A,i}+(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}\mathbf{X}_{A^{c},i}-(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1})_{A}+(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\ast}_{1})_{A^{c}}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A,A}\mathbf{E}_{A,i}.

where the last equality uses the regression relationship (
10
).
This completes the derivation of the second identity.

F.2.3
Estimation error bound for the ISEE subroutine

We derive the estimation error bound presented in (
12
) in Section
3.2.2
, recalled below:

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-

\displaystyle(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)-\bigl(({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}-({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}\lesssim C_{6}\max\{J\log p/n,\sqrt{\log p/n}\},

where
J
is the maximum number of nonzero off-diagonal entries per row of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}
,
and
C_{6}=\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
.

We start by using the triangle inequality to obtain the following bound:

\bigl\|\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)-\bigl(({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}-({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}~\leq~\|(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}-(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}.

We can bound the
\|(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
term as follows:

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})

{}_{A_{\ell}}-(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\;\overset{(i)}{=}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\;\overset{(ii)}{=}\bigl\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}~+~\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\;\;=\bigl\|\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}^{\ast}\bigr)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}~+~\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}^{\ast}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\;\;\leq\bigl\|\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}^{\ast}\bigr)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr\|_{2}~+~\bigl\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}^{\ast}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\;\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}~+~\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2},

where step
(i)
uses the key identity from equation (
11
) and the fact that the ISEE subroutine employs a plug-in estimator,
step
(ii)
adds and subtracts the term
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}
, and
step
(iii)
applies the operator norm bound
\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
.
We can also derive a similar bound for
\|(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}-(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
.
Therefore we have:

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-

\displaystyle(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)-\bigl(({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}-({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}~+~\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}~+~\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\;\|(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}.

Under some regularity conditions,

Fan and Lv, 2016
proves that

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
,

\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}
,
and

\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}-(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}

all have
have convergence rates of order

\max\{J\log p/n,\sqrt{\log p/n}\}.

Therefore, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}-(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)

\displaystyle-\bigl(({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}-({\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{\ast})_{A_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigl(\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+\|(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\|_{2}+2\|\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\|_{2}\bigr)\max\biggl\{\frac{J\log p}{n},\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{6}\max\biggl\{\frac{J\log p}{n},\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}\biggr\}.

This completes the estimation error bound presented in (
12
).

Algorithm 4

ISEE subroutine:
ISEE

1:

Data matrix
\mathbf{X}
, cluster estimate
\hat{G}_{1},\hat{G}_{2}

2:

A_{1},\ldots,A_{L}\leftarrow
non-overlapping subsets of
[p]
of size 2 or 3

3:

for

\ell=1,2,\ldots L

do

4:

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\ell}\leftarrow\{(\mathbf{X}_{A_{\ell}^{c},i},\mathbf{X}_{A_{\ell},i}):i\in\hat{G}_{1}\}

5:

(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}},\{\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},i}\}_{i\in\hat{G}_{1}}\leftarrow
slope and residuals obtained from running lasso on
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\ell}

6:

\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\ell}\leftarrow\{(\mathbf{X}_{A_{\ell}^{c},i},\mathbf{X}_{A_{\ell},i}):i\in\hat{G}_{2}\}

7:

(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}},\{\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},i}\}_{i\in\hat{G}_{2}}\leftarrow
slope and residuals obtained from running lasso on
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\ell}

8:

\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}\leftarrow(\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},n})

\triangleright
Residual block

9:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\leftarrow(\frac{1}{n}\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}^{\top})^{-1},

\triangleright
Precision matrix block

10:

(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}\leftarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}},

\triangleright
Transformed cluster 1 center block

11:

(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}\leftarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}

\triangleright
Transformed cluster 2 center block

12:

\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{M}}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}\leftarrow
an
|A_{\ell}|\times n
matrix where the
i
th column is
(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{\ell}}
if
i\in\hat{G}_{1}
, and
(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{\ell}}
if
i\in\hat{G}_{2}

13:

\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}\leftarrow\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{M}}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{A_{\ell},A_{\ell}}\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{A_{\ell},\cdot}

\triangleright
Transformed data block

14:

\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}\leftarrow\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{1}}^{\top},\ldots,(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1})_{A_{L}}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top},

\triangleright
Aggregate cluster 1 center blocks

15:

\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}\leftarrow\bigl((\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{1}}^{\top},\ldots,(\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2})_{A_{L}}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top}

\triangleright
Aggregate cluster 2 center blocks

16:

\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}\leftarrow\bigl(\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{A_{1},\cdot}^{\top},\ldots,\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}_{A_{L},\cdot}^{\top}\bigr)^{\top}

\triangleright
Aggregate transformed data blocks

17:

Output
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1},\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2},\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}

Appendix G
Supplementary details for numerical study

This section provides additional details on the numerical studies, omitted from Section
4
.
Appendix
G.1
provides details on parameter tuning, early stopping criterion, implementation details, and computing resources.
Appendix
G.2
describes sparsity-aware high-dimensional clustering methods used for comparison.
Appendix
G.3
presents two additional simulation results: 1) numerical evidence supporting the convergence of our algorithm under the proposed stopping criterion, and 2) evaluation of Algorithm
2
under known non-isotropic covariance.

G.1
Supplementary details for the simulations

This section provides details on hyperparameter tuning, software implementation, early stopping criteria, and computing resources related to the simulation study presented in Section
4
.

Parameter tuning for lasso.

For regularization parameter tuning in the ISEE subroutine (Algorithm
4
) at each iteration of Algorithm
3
, we use the lasso path algorithm to efficiently explore a range of regularization values and select the one that minimizes the Akaike Information Criterion (AIC). This procedure is implemented using functions from the MATLAB package
penalized

(
McIlhagga, 2016
)
.

Early stopping.

For efficiency, we employ a data-driven stopping criterion to terminate the algorithm before reaching the
T
th iteration.
At each iteration
t
, the stopping condition is met if either of the following conditions is met:
(1) the relative change in the objective value falls below
\pi\%
; or
(2) after a warm-up period of
w
iterations, the best objective value observed shows less than
\pi\%
improvement over the most recent
\eta
iterations. The algorithm terminates only when this condition holds for both the SDP objective in SDP K-means subroutine (line 1 of Algorithm
1
) and the original K-means objective. Unless stated otherwise, we set
w=10
,
\eta=5
, and
\pi=1\%
in our experiments. Appendix
G.3
provides a numerical evaluation of this stopping criterion.

Software implementation.

Our algorithms are implemented in MATLAB, and all simulations are run using version R2023b. To solve the SDP problems, we use the ADMM-based solver SDPNAL++
(
Sun et al., 2020
)
, which scales efficiently for sample sizes up to
n<5000
. In the final step of the SDP-relaxed K-means, we extract the leading eigenvector (an
n
-dimensional vector) from the output matrix
Z
and apply the
kmeans
function from MATLAB Statistics and Machine Learning Toolbox. For the ISEE subroutine, we utilize the MATLAB Parallel Computing Toolbox to simulataeneously estimate the cluster centers and precision matrix blocks. For graphical lasso in Algorithm
5
, we use the implementation provided by
Leung et al., 2016
. In Scenario 1, hierarchical clustering is performed using the
linkage
function with default options, also from MATLAB Statistics and Machine Learning Toolbox. When comparing against baseline methods, we use their original implementations, some in MATLAB and others in R, executed within their respective environments. All implementations and simulation codes, including those for the baseline methods written by their original authors, are available at
our GitHub repository
.

Computing resources.

All simulations are conducted on a Linux machine equipped with an AMD EPYC 7542 processor (32 cores, 2.90 GHz) and 16 GB of RAM. A single run of Algorithm
3
, from initialization to the final iteration, completes in under one minute on this system.

G.2
Sparsity-aware clustering methods used for benchmarking

This section provides details of the baseline sparsity-aware methods that were compared to our algorithms in Section
4
.

IF-PCA.

Influential Feature PCA (IF-PCA;
Jin and Wang, 2016
) is a two step method.
In the variable selection step, features with the highest Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) scores are selected, with the selection threshold determined in a data-driven manner using the Higher Criticism principle
(
Donoho and Jin, 2004
)
.
In the clustering step, spectral clustering is applied: the top
K-1
left singular vectors are extracted from the normalized data matrix after feature selection and used as inputs to the classical
K
-means algorithm to estimate cluster labels. For simulations, we use the authors’
MATLAB implementation
.

SAS.

Sparse Alternate Similarity clustering (SAS;
Arias-Castro and Pu, 2017
) is an iterative method that uses the fact that the within-cluster dissimilarity of K-means, measured by the squared
\ell_{2}
distances, can be decomposed coordinate-wise. The algorithm alternates between clustering and support estimation: given a support set, it applies K-means to estimate the clusters; given the cluster assignments, it selects the top
\hat{|S_{0}|}
coordinates with the largest average within-cluster dissimilarity. The tuning parameter
\hat{|S_{0}|}
, which denotes the number of signal features, is selected using the following permutation-based approach: For each
\hat{|S_{0}|}=1,\ldots,p
, run the SAS algorithm to obtain corresponding cluster and support estimates. For each run, compare the within-cluster dissimilarity of the selected features under the estimated cluster assignment to that under a randomly permuted cluster assignment. The discrepancy between the two is quantified using the gap statistic
(
Tibshirani et al., 2001
)
. Then choose the value of
\hat{|S_{0}|}
that yields the largest gap statistic.
We use the authors’
R implementation
.

Sparse K-means.

Witten and Tibshirani, 2010
proposes Sparse K-means, an iterative method which exploits the fact that the within-cluster dissimilarity in K-means, measured by squared
\ell_{2}
distances, can be decomposed coordinate-wise. The method assigns a feature weight
w_{j}
to each variable
j=1,\ldots,p
, and constructs a feature-weighted version of the K-means objective. It then optimizes this objective over both feature weights and cluster assignments, subject to a combined lasso and ridge penalty. The proposed algorithm alternates between maximizing the objective with respect to the cluster assignments (given fixed feature weights) and with respect to the feature weights (given fixed cluster assignments).
For simulations, we use the author’s
R package
sparcl
.

CHIME.

CHIME
(
Cai et al., 2019
)
is an EM-type iterative method, where the E-step updates the cluster assignments based on current Gaussian mixture model parameters, and the M-step updates the model parameters with cluster labels fixed.
Notably, CHIME avoids explicit covariance estimation by employing an
\ell_{1}
-regularized estimation of the sparse discriminative direction
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\ast}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{\ast})
.
After a suitable number of iterations, the estimated
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
is plugged into the Fisher discriminant rule to produce the final cluster assignments.
For simulations, we use the authors’
MATLAB implementation
.

Algorithm 5

Iterative SDP K-means with unknown covariance using graphical lasso

1:

Data matrix
\mathbf{X}
, maximum number of iteration
T

2:

Let
\hat{G}_{1}^{0},\hat{G}_{2}^{0}
be cluster estimates obtained by running spectral clustering on
\mathbf{X}

3:

for
t = 1, 2, …
T

do

4:

\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{1}^{t}\leftarrow|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}\mathbf{X}_{i}

5:

\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{2}^{t}\leftarrow|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}\mathbf{X}_{i}

6:

\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{t}\leftarrow\bigl(\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}-\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{1}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}^{\top},\;\mathbf{X}_{\cdot,\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}-\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{2}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}^{\top}\bigr)

\triangleright
Cluster-wise demeaned data matrix

7:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t}\leftarrow\texttt{GraphicalLasso}\bigl(\dot{\mathbf{X}}^{t}\bigr)

8:

\hat{\omega}_{11}^{t},\ldots\hat{\omega}_{pp}^{t}\leftarrow\mathrm{diag}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t})

9:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{t}\leftarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t}\bigl(\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{1}^{t}-\hat{\bar{\mathbf{X}}}_{2}^{t}\bigr)

10:

\hat{S}^{t}\leftarrow\left\{j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:|\hat{\beta}^{t}_{j}|>\sqrt{2\hat{\omega}_{jj}^{t}n\log(2p)}/\sqrt{|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}|\cdot|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}|}\right\}

\triangleright
Variable selection

11:

\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}\leftarrow\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t}\mathbf{X}

12:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\leftarrow\bigl((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t})^{-1}\bigr)_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}

13:

\hat{G}_{1}^{t},\hat{G}_{2}^{t}\leftarrow\texttt{SDPkmeans}(\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot},\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}})

\triangleright
Clustering (Algorithm
1
)

14:

Output
\hat{G}^{T}_{1},\hat{G}^{T}_{2}

Next, to assess the efficacy of Algorithm
3
, which avoids full precision matrix estimation and uses a cutoff based on the convergence rate of ISEE,
we introduce two modified variants.

Algorithm
5
: Algorithm
3
with graphical lasso.

At each iteration
t
, Algorithm
5
fully estimates the precision matrix using graphical lasso
(
Friedman et al., 2008
)
, and uses it to compute the necessary quantities:
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}
. These estimates are then used in variable selection and clustering steps.

Algorithm
6
: Algorithm
3
with threshold based on Gaussian maximal inequality.

The estimated submatrix blocks of the precision matrix, computed in line 8 of Algorithm
4
, also provide precision matrix diagonal estimates, denoted
\hat{\omega}_{11},\ldots,\hat{\omega}_{pp}
.
Algorithm
6
leverages these diagonal estimates to compute plug-in estimates for the quantities required by Algorithm
3
,
instead of using
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{1}^{t}
and
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{2}^{t}
.

Algorithm 6

Iterative SDP K-means with unknown covariance using threshold based on Gaussian maximal inequality

1:

Data matrix
\mathbf{X}
, maximum number of iteration
T

2:

Let
\hat{G}_{1}^{0},\hat{G}_{2}^{0}
be cluster estimates obtained by running spectral clustering on
\mathbf{X}

3:

for
t = 1, 2, …
T

do

4:

\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t},\omega_{11}^{t},\ldots,\omega_{pp}^{t}\leftarrow\texttt{ISEE}(\mathbf{X},\hat{G}_{1}^{t-1},\hat{G}_{2}^{t-1})
\triangleright
Algorithm
4

modified to output
\hat{\omega}_{11}^{t},\ldots,\hat{\omega}_{pp}^{t}

5:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{t}\leftarrow|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}_{i}-|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}_{i}.

6:

\hat{S}^{t}\leftarrow\left\{j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}:|\hat{\beta}^{t}_{j}|>\sqrt{2\hat{\omega}_{jj}^{t}n\log(2p)}/\sqrt{|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}|\cdot|\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}|}\right\}

\triangleright
Variable selection

7:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}}\leftarrow

pooled covariance of covariances computed from
\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{1}}
and
\mathbf{X}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{G}^{t-1}_{2}}

8:

\hat{G}_{1}^{t},\hat{G}_{2}^{t}\leftarrow\texttt{SDPkmeans}(\hat{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\cdot},\;\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t}_{\hat{S}^{t},\hat{S}^{t}})

\triangleright
Clustering (Algorithm
1
)

9:

Output
\hat{G}_{1}^{T},\hat{G}_{2}^{T}

G.3
Additional simulation results

This section presents two additional simulation results.

G.3.1
Numerical Evidence Supporting the Convergence of Our Algorithm

We provide numerical evidence supporting the convergence of Algorithm
3
under the stopping criterion presented in Appendix
G.1
.
We re-use the setup from Scenario 2 of Section
4
, speicifically the scenario
(p,\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}},\rho)=(400,4,0.45)
.
Line 1 of Table
3
presents the details of the setting.
The stopping criteria are applied using the hyperparameters
T=100
,
\pi=0.5\%
,
w=40
, and
\eta=20
.
We conduct 200 independent replications to record when the iterative algorithm
3
terminates.
At each stopping point, we record the mis-clustering rate, the number of true positives (signal entries retained), and false positives (noise entries retained). The results in Figure
3
reveal two main insights about the behavior of our stopping criteria and the performance of Algorithm
3
:
First, the stopping criteria are effective. Figure
3
-(a) shows that the algorithms consistently stop before reaching the maximum iteration limit, providing empirical evidence of convergence. Across different estimation-cutoff strategies, the stopping points are similarly concentrated.
Second, true discoveries contribute more to clustering performance than the avoidance of false ones. As shown in Figure
3
-(b), the original Algorithm
3
demonstrates more stable clustering performance compared to its variant, which often yields mis-clustering rates near 0.5. Figures
3
-(c) and
3
-(d) shows that the original algorithm makes slightly more true discoveries and incurs many more false discoveries, respectively.

and its variants shown in Figure

. The data setting follows line 1 of Table
3
, and the stopping criteria are applied using the hyperparameters
T=100
,
\pi=0.5\%
,
w=40
, and
\eta=20
.

G.3.2
Evaluation of Algorithm
2
under known non-isotropic covariance

Under the known AR(1) covariance setting, we numerically validate that Algorithm
2
effectively adapts to sparsity of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\ast}
and examine how varying correlation levels impact clustering.
The data generation setting is the same as in Section
4
, with details specified in line 2 of Table
3
. The result presented in Figure
4
highlights two key findings. First, the iterative algorithm successfully adapts to sparsity of discriminative direction: its performance remains stable (especially in
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\ast}}=4
) across increasing ambient dimensions
p
. Second, when the covariance structure is known, higher correlation improves clustering. As
\rho
increases, mis-clustering rates degrade more slowly with
p
due to the redundancy in noise.

Figure 4:
Mis-clustering rates of Algorithm
2
with spectral initialization under the AR(1) correlation structures for
\rho=0.2,0.5,0.8
. The data generation setting is the same as in Section
4
, with details specified in line 2 of Table
3
. The dotted line of the same color represents the mis-clustering rate of spectral initialization under the same covariance structure.
